HEY met in a
THEY
PART ONE
1900
I
candy shop," said Mrs. King.
She was referring to her son Robert and the girl who
had been his wife for the last four months, but had she said
they met in "an African jungle" or "a Chinese opium den,"
her voice couldn't have conveyed more well-bred distaste of
the situation. Helen Langdon, lifelong friend, just back from
a year abroad, raised eyes which held a blending of sympathy
and amusement as she responded: "You mean-"”
"I mean," broke in Susan King as if urged by an imperative
need to get the story out of her system without delay, "that
she was behind the counter. Think of it, Helen! And it
wasn't Huyler's, you understand, where the salesgirls are, as
a rule, quite ladylike. It was a cheap little shop on lower
Washington Street, wedged in between a cigar store and a
men's furnishing place. She actually waited on him!"
"Indeed?" murmured Miss Langdon. "But what in the
world, Sue, induced a son of yours to venture into a shop
like that?"
Her old friend drew a long, sad breath.
"Fate," she responded. "An unkind fate; and a low, child-
ish taste, inherited, I fear, from my uncle by marriage, Jere-
miah Caswell. You will remember-”
"Can one inherit by marriage?" questioned Helen, a smile
deepening the amusement in her eyes; while Mrs. King re-
plied a trifle sharply:
3
4
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
"This is no time to joke, Helen, or to pick one up. I meant,
of course, Uncle Jeremiah's example. The family always felt
that Aunt Susan (I was her namesake) married beneath her,
and that Jeremiah Caswell had no ambition. He owned a
farm in Chelmsford, you know, near Lowell; and though
through Father's influence he was offered a good position in
the Mechanics Bank, he had the face to refuse it, which caused
a coolness to arise between them."
For the moment Mrs. King had forgotten her present
trouble, being lost in one long past; and Miss Langdon, some-
what puzzled, prompted: "But, Susan, what has this to do
with Robert and a Washington Street candy store?"
"Everything," replied the young man's mother, gloom
settling over her again. "You see, when they were children,
Robert and his cousin, Lester Hale (at that age Lester showed
no signs of the disgrace he was to become later), used to visit
at Aunt Susan's; and it seems that Rob never forgot the place.
The chief attraction was, I believe, a goat, for which Uncle
Jeremiah made a little harness. The boys drove the creature,
hitched to a small express cart with red wheels. Chelmsford
was only a sleepy village then, and Uncle Jeremiah, who
never had a particle of sense, bought candy for them at the
grocery store. It's a wonder Robert survived it a high-strung
child such as he was, with a delicate stomach; and there was
one kind he liked particularly, stick licorice, they called it,
light brown in color with-"
"Ridges running up and down like Doric columns!" ex-
claimed Miss Langdon. "I haven't seen any for years. I
wonder if Bob's got some left. I'd like- Oh, excuse me,
Sue. I didn't mean to interrupt. I suppose Robert saw the
licorice in this shop window and—"
Susan King nodded a bit irritably, disliking above all things
to have a story taken out of her mouth, as it were; and for
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
5
months she'd been waiting to pour this one in all its distress-
ing details over her lifelong and trusted friend.
"Yes," she continued. "It was in a glass jar, beside sticks
of old-fashioned red and white peppermint candy. As Robert
explained, it 'brought dear old Uncle Jeremiah back,' and he
felt he just must try the stuff again and see if it tasted as good
as it did when he was seven; and Sandra (he calls her 'Sandy,'
Helen, which sounds to me like nothing under heaven but a
pet dog) was behind the counter."
Mrs. King paused to draw another long, sad breath. She
even closed her eyes a moment as if to shut out the picture of
her youngest son, Harvard 1896, son of Harvard 1865, grand-
son of Harvard 1840, making free with a salesgirl in a cheap
candy shop. Indeed, so lost was she in contemplating this
distressing picture, that Miss Langdon was obliged to prompt
again, saying: "Well, Susan?" before her friend went on:
"The girl was alone in the store. If she hadn't been, or if
other customers had come in just then, all might have been
avoided. But she was alone, and (I simply can't imagine what
got into him) Robert told her why he wanted the licorice.
And it seems she laughed and said she used to love it, too;
and did he remember when they sold caramels two for a
cent? Of course he said he did, though where, unless Uncle
Jeremiah was to blame again, he ever saw such things, I
can't conceive; and well, that was the beginning. Did you
ever hear anything more-more bourgeois?"
If a fugitive smile hovered for a moment on Miss Langdon's
lips, it was quickly suppressed as she responded: "But we must
remember, Sue, that customs change with the generations.
Perhaps-"
"Customs never change in the conduct of ladies and gentle-
men," broke in Mrs. King reprovingly. "What would your
own dear mother have thought, Helen, if you had chatted
familiarly with a clerk behind a counter?"
6
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
Helen arose as if attracted by some sound outside, and go-
ing to a window, pushed back velvet draperies to look out
through glass of palest purple at the bare elms on the Com-
mon, not because she wished to see them, but because there
were moments when Mrs. King's dark eyes had a discon-
certing effect, as if they could plumb one's very depths. The
year was 1900, and as she moved across a Persian rug there
was a gratifying rustle of taffeta petticoats; and her old friend
noticed how evenly the much-gored broadcloth skirt rippled
behind her. Paris, of course. Helen was, she mused, still
extravagant when it came to clothes. Even abroad that lace
jabot had cost a pretty penny. Only yesterday she'd priced
one similar at Hollander's.
...
"What do you hear?" she questioned, rather abruptly.
Roused from a reverie, Miss Langdon started, and dropped
the draperies behind her, wondering inwardly what Susan
King would think if told that what she'd heard was the voice
of a brakeman on the Boston and Albany Railroad saying,
"Nice morning, isn't it?" as she left the train at the Hunting-
ton Avenue station many, many years ago. That was the
time when because of her mother's frail health, they had
spent a year in the country near Framingham, and Helen,
then a delightfully pretty seventeen, had commuted to her
Back Bay school.
The brakeman, a handsome, brown-eyed fellow, had once
picked up the glove she had dropped for just that purpose
as she stepped off the last car of the train; and she had
thanked him a trifle breathlessly, noticing that before he spoke
the young man had removed his cap in a most gentlemanly
manner-how gorgeous his eyes were the way his hair
waved back from his forehead, and well, that first hurried
thank-you was only the beginning; and though Helen never
confessed it even to the girls at school, she had enjoyed a
genuine "crush" on that B. and A. brakeman. As for "chat-
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
7
ting"-would Susan consider the few daring words spoken
on the back platform of that early train, "chatting"? she won-
dered. With a smile for the past, Miss Langdon returned to
the present and her friend's divan.
"I thought I heard voices, Sue. Go on with your story.
How long did it take dear Robert to fall in love?"
"Love!" bristled Mrs. King. "Call it by its right name,
Helen-infatuation. I understand that it took him about five
minutes-Robert-who might have any girl in the Vincent
Club just for the asking!" If Miss Langdon doubted this
extravagant statement she made no sign; and after a moment
Mrs. King continued: "At any rate, he developed an astound-
ing passion for stick licorice. I found-attracted by the un-
pleasant odor-literally pounds of it, my dear, stuffed into a
drawer with his dress shirts. I had to air them for days, and
send one to the laundry."
A laugh escaped Miss Langdon this time; then, meeting
Mrs. King's reproachful eyes, she gave her hand a contrite
squeeze.
"Forgive me, Sue. This is no laughing matter to you, of
course. But tell me aside from her humble occupation,
what's wrong with the girl?"
Slowly, as if the occasion demanded dignity, Mrs. King
arose, went to a Chippendale desk across the room, unlocked
a drawer, and returned bearing a letter which she extended
gingerly, almost as if the object were unclean. The note
paper was pink, a pale pink, to be sure, but nevertheless, pink.
"Read that," she said.
With considerable interest Helen slipped the letter from the
envelope, noticing that its rough edges were lightly flecked
with gold. There was, she also noticed, a good deal of char-
acter about the handwriting. Evidently the note was a reply
to one from Robert's mother, written after the young man
had confessed his engagement. It said:
8
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
My dear Mrs. King: It was very kind of you to write me.
You couldn't of done anything I would have liked more. I
do hope we shall be friends for Robert's sake. He is such a
dear, and if we two shouldn't get along he would be unhappy.
I hope I shall have the pleasure of meeting you soon.
Sincerely and gratefully yours,
Sandra Teresa Bowen.
Having given her friend time to peruse this short epistle
more than once, Mrs. King ventured sternly: "Well?" Then
as Miss Langdon remained silent, she quoted: "You couldn't
of! Think of it, Helen! My Robert's wife saying 'you
couldn't of! Why don't you speak, my dear? What are
you thinking about?"
What Helen Langdon was thinking about was another note
written on the impeccable stationery of the Harvard Club.
It had reached her just before sailing, and was, like this other,
brief in the extreme, merely saying:
Dear Aunt Helen: For the love of Mike, when you get
back make Mother see daylight. You'll get an earful on
landing. Sandy's an angel, but her wings are beginning to
droop; and to live “twixt love and duty" is ruining my once
superb physique! Joking aside, I'm counting on you, Aunt
Helen. Excuse haste, but this must catch the boat. Yours
often happily, more often unhappily, always desperately, Bob.
Still holding the pink envelope, Miss Langdon raised her
eyes.
"But," she hesitated, "aside from that one small error, Sue-"
"Small error!" broke in the outraged Mrs. King, "could any
error be much worse? One could forgive a misspelled word,
Helen. It's well known that even President Eliot occasionally
misspells. But to- And that excruciating note paper!" she
exploded suddenly.
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
9
Helen Langdon was silent. Then: "Did you-er-show it
to Robert?"י
Her friend nodded.
"I hoped-I prayed, my dear, that it would open his eyes;
but he only smiled-the most enigmatic smile, Helen, and
when I called his attention to the gilt edges he grew disagree-
ably sarcastic. He said, 'It's my fault, Mother. I should have
warned Sandy to use paper with the Bowen crest engraved
at the top.' Imagine Robert speaking to me like that!"
It did sound a trifle sharp for Robert, who was usually
sweet-tempered. "He must have been goaded," thought Helen
Langdon; and asked: "What is her family, my dear? What
sort of people are they, I mean?"
Diamonds, which were left at Bigelow and Kennard's each
week for cleaning, flashed in the dim light from those cur-
tained windows as Mrs. King made a gesture of protest.
"Nobodies!" she moaned. "Worse than nobodies.
Wouldn't you guess it from that note paper? The girl has
no background, Helen, none whatever; and positively no
interests outside her home and and Robert."
Miss Langdon pondered this before observing: "She might
have worse interests than a home and husband, Sue; and
surely she has background of some sort. Everyone has."
"You know perfectly well what I mean," snapped Susan
King, surprise and irritated that her lifelong crony should
appear to be looking for a bright side of this devastating
trouble; "but if you call a plumber a background, my dear,
why Sandra has one. I understand that her father cleans out
sink drains or worse. They live in Roxbury, some dreadful
run-down portion of the place. Her mother (thank God!)
is dead; but her brother is a conductor on the electric cars,
and she has a sister who does manicuring in a-a barber
shop!"
This was, Helen mentally admitted, a bitter dose for a
10
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
King of Beacon Hill to swallow. And nothing-not one thing
in Susan's life had prepared her for it. She was well born-
had married even better; and there had been only rejoicing
when her first son chose to spend his life with a Frothing-
ham, and the second with a Peabody of Cambridge. And
here was Robert, the youngest, the very apple of his mother's
eye, securely married to the daughter of a Roxbury plumber!
Suddenly a wave of understanding sympathy for her old
friend swept over Helen Langdon. She said gently:
"I'm sorry, Sue, sorry as can be. But let's go back a way.
When was it that Robert first saw the girl?"
"In May, my dear."
"May!" echoed Helen. "And in the spring a young man's
fancy lightly turns-'"
"Not lightly in this case," broke in Mrs. King. "There was
nothing light about it, I assure you. It was on a Wednesday
they met (if one can call it meeting), and only a week from
the next Saturday he drove her out into the country to see
the apple blossoms, which were at their height."
"Apple blossoms!" breathed Helen. "What an unfortunate
time of year!"
"It was," agreed the young man's mother, taking this liter-
ally, as, indeed, she took everything. "And Sunday they
went down the harbor on a boat, to Nantasket, probably, or
some other impossible resort. I had no idea whom the boy
was with, of course, or I'd have made an attempt to nip things
in the bud-to point out that nothing but misery could come
of such an entanglement. And only a month later he told
me he was engaged to her!"
"That must have taken courage on Bob's part," mused
Miss Langdon; and said: "Is the girl a Catholic?"
"No. I'm spared that, though it would have made no dif-
ference to Robert. (Imagine my grandchildren, the blood of
Unitarianism boiling in their veins, being brought up by the
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
11
Pope!) They were married in July at the City Hall, or
wherever people go to get married when they don't do it
decently in church. And they had no honeymoon because
Robert couldn't get away just then. They went right to the
old house on West Cedar Street."
"Bob owns it, doesn't he?"
"Yes. Avery deeded it to Robert when he bought the house
on Commonwealth Avenue, because Rob was always so fond
of the old place. It'll soon be in the slums, but that makes
no difference. Perhaps his wife will feel all the more at home
there," said Mrs. King with bitterness.
"Oh, Susan!” her friend protested, "don't speak like that!
There must be fine qualities of some sort in the girl, or a
splendid boy like Robert wouldn't love her. And just think
how fortunate you are to have two of your three sons marry
so well. Tell me how James and Stephen take this thing."
"Take it? Like the gentlemen they are, of course. James
was horrified, but he and Dorothy called promptly. Stephen
was, I think, inclined to feel that whom one marries is strictly
one's own affair. He's easy-going, you know, like his uncle
Avery. I understand that when I telephoned the news he
rushed right down to Robert's office and well, not congratu-
lated him, I suppose (one could hardly do that), but wished
him joy. And before they'd been married three days Laura
went round one morning and called on Sandra. She found
her-you'll hardly believe it, Helen-ironing a starched white
petticoat!"
"Heaven help her!" exclaimed Miss Langdon. "My last
were four yards round! What possessed Laura to go in the
morning, Sue? If she were a spiteful person I'd say she was
taking a mean advantage."
"Not at all. She considered it a family call; and Laura's
impulsive. She said she couldn't wait an hour longer. When
Sandra found it was Robert's sister-in-law, she asked her into
12
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
the kitchen while she finished the petticoat. It seems her
sister needed it that afternoon, and the irons were just right
or something. Laura said the girl didn't appear at all in awe
of her; though of course dear Laura, being a lady, didn't put
it just that way."
"She wouldn't," observed Helen, who was fond of Stephen
King's young wife. Then: "Have you called on her people,
Susan?"
"No," said Mrs. King. "I decided that such a call would
be too painful for all concerned. Even Robert agreed that
since Sandra had no mother, it wasn't necessary. However,
I invited her to dinner-a family affair, with just the boys and
their wives, you understand. It almost killed me. Robert
said to make it simple, 'soup,' he suggested, 'and a roast, and
just ice cream,' but I was determined to do nothing the girl
could resent. We had nine courses and the best glass and
silver. I'll have to admit, my dear, that only once did I see
her take up the wrong fork and put it down again. As Doro-
thy remarked later, she kept her eyes open."
"Which is half the battle, isn't it?" returned Helen. "If I
were you, Sue, I'd-"
But this advice was interrupted by a white-capped maid
announcing: "Luncheon is served, Mrs. King."
II
THE clock in Park Street church was chiming four when
Helen Langdon bade her old friend an affectionate good-bye,
promising to return with her for dinner after the Symphony
rehearsal on Friday afternoon. She had refused to allow the
King coachman to drive her home, pleading a genuine need
of fresh air and exercise; for Susan had talked of almost
nothing save her son's "misalliance"-had, in fact, told and
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
13
retold each distressing detail so many times that Helen's
usually placid nerves were on edge and she wondered if she
didn't know more about Bob King's love affair than the poor
boy did himself!
Once out on the brick sidewalk she drew a deep breath of
pleasure as she looked about. It was good to be back. After
all, there was nothing in the world quite like Boston-Beacon
Street-the State House dome lighting a wintry sky. "In a
word," reflected Miss Langdon as she crossed to the Common,
"there's no place like home, and no friends like the old ones,
even if they're a trifle irritating at times!"
She had meant to do an errand at Stearns's before returning
to her Marlborough Street house, not because the errand was
urgent, but because going to Stearns's, if only for some stock-
ings or a pair of blue silk garters, was part of her homecom-
ing; but, pausing to observe the familiar sight of an old man
feeding the pigeons, Helen suddenly decided that Stearns's
could wait, and that instead she'd call on Robert King's new
wife. To be sure, Susan had offered to take her some day
soon, but she wanted to go alone-to see for herself if Bob
was merely infatuated with a pretty face, and if his mother
was right when she proclaimed that "nothing but misery
could come of the entanglement."
As Miss Langdon turned toward a path that led past the
frog pond, big snowflakes were beginning to float down, which
added distinctly to her pleasure. Having spent the previous
winter in southern Italy, snow seemed not only a novelty, but
a very part of dear New England; and when after a brisk
walk she approached the house on West Cedar Street her
thoughts were drifting back to her own youth and the happy
hours she had spent beneath its hospitable roof.
How long ago that was-and how times changed, she
mused. After Sue's parents died her brother Avery had lived
in the old home until nagged out of it by a wife who felt
14
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
no sentiment for the house where her husband was born, and
saw only the encroaching "slums" which Susan had spoken
of that morning. But to Helen Langdon the neighborhood
was reminiscent of those days when, returning after some gay
party to spend the night with Sue, the two girls had giggled
or whispered until dawn; and, like Avery Hale, she felt a
keen affection for the very street. As to the house itself, its
fine old doorway had stood the test of time; and as she
touched the bell Miss Langdon wondered if Bob's wife had
made any unwelcome changes within. Did the red Turkey
carpet still run through the lower hall and upstairs into the
parlors on the second floor? Perhaps it was too shabby by
this time, but it had showed amazingly little wear eight years
ago when last she'd lunched here with Etta Hale. Helen
rang again, decided no one was in, and was just about to
peer boldly through the sidelights (being still curious about
the Turkey carpet) when hurried footsteps reached her ears
and the door was opened by a flushed and breathless young
woman with an apology on her lips, and just a shade of
embarrassment in her manner.
"I'm so sorry to keep you standing here, but I was up four
flights when the bell rang, and had to stop to wash my hands
on the way down. You're Miss Langdon, aren't you? I've
seen your picture; and Bob said you'd be certain to drop in
right away."
"I'm glad he felt so sure I'd come," smiled Helen as she
stepped inside. "I've always had a warm corner in my heart
for Robert ever since the day he was born when his mother's
nurse let me hold him for five minutes. He was a darling
baby."
"I'll bet he was!" returned Robert's wife, forgetting her
embarrassment for a moment; then colored as she amended:
"I mean it would be hard to imagine Bob not darling at
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
15
any age, though he says I'm a prejudiced party! Let's go
upstairs. There's a log fire in the sitting room.”
Helen smiled. She was looking down at the red carpet,
and her hostess said: "I wanted to take that old thing up
and put down a good straw matting. I told Bob it would be
so much easier to sweep; but he said it had been here ever
since he could remember, and wouldn't let me touch it. He
said" (a smile curved the girl's mouth) "he actually said he'd
rather do the sweeping himself than see it go."
"Good for Bob!" laughed Helen. "That carpet was here
before he was born, my dear. I remember when his grand-
father bought it in New York, and how wonderful everybody
thought it was.”
She had started up the stairs, but at these words the bride
stopped her with a gesture.
"Perhaps," she began, hesitating a little, “perhaps as you
remember the house so well you'd like to see it all. We
haven't done very much, but it does look nice and neat, I
think. Come into my kitchen."
"I love kitchens," responded Miss Langdon, and added, as
Sandra opened a door at the end of the passage, "Why, how
lovely!"
Instantly the girl seemed to glow with pleasure. "Isn't it?"
she exclaimed; while Helen, who had decided that she wasn't
pretty, changed her mind. "Do you remember how forlorn
it was such a small window-that awful wooden sink, and
dingy walls?"
The caller nodded thoughtfully.
"Yes, I remember, though I didn't see it often. People
never paid much attention to kitchens when I was young.
Everyone everyone we knew, I mean-kept servants; and
I'm afraid we didn't lose much sleep over their comfort, either.
And of course there wasn't any such fine plumbing then; and
none at all, perhaps, when this old house was built."
16
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
She glanced admiringly at the spotless sink with its shining
faucets, and the girl explained: "That lovely plumbing was
part of a wedding present from my father. He-" She
stopped abruptly, flushing, but went on almost at once with
what Helen recognized as courage, "he's in the business, or
he couldn't have given us anything so fine. He put it in
himself, and the new bathroom you'll see upstairs. Wasn't
it dear of him?"
"Indeed it was!" said Helen warmly, even as she thought:
"This is why she took Laura into the kitchen, poor child!
She wanted her to see that sink. As if Laura King would
notice."
"Do you like the walls?" Sandra was asking. "Bob thought
the yellow would look cheerful. I-I painted them myself."
"Yourself!"
A laugh came from Sandra at the incredulity in Miss Lang-
don's voice.
"Oh, I wasn't supposed to! But the painter didn't come
the day he promised, and I was so mad I started doing it,
and when Bob came home at night the room was finished.
You ought to have seen his face when he found out!"
Helen could imagine it. The girls Robert King had known
might, possibly, have painted a little china (hand-painted
china being quite the vogue), but kitchen walls- Yes, she
could imagine Robert's face! She said, smiling: "Well, I
think you're clever. I couldn't have done it to save my soul.
And did you make the curtains, too?"
They were yellow organdie with narrow ruffles, reminding
Helen vaguely of dresses she'd worn to children's parties when
a little girl.
"No; my sister made those for me, and Bob says they're
grand. I love a window over the sink, don't you? And the
back yard's so pretty here. I can hardly do my dishes because
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
17
there's always something to watch outside. At home there
were only board fences and other people's rubbish."
Miss Langdon said: "It would be hard to get inspiration
from a rubbish barrel! Have you altered the dining room?"
As swiftly as the girl had glowed a moment since, she
changed, "as if a little cloud had crossed the sun," Helen was
thinking. They were going through the newly-painted but-
ler's pantry when she stopped to answer: "I have, a little;
and though he's far too dear to say so, I feel, somehow, that
Bob doesn't like it. You see, Miss Langdon, my aunt sent
me some money to buy a wedding present, and I saw this
dining room set in a secondhand store. It was a real bar-
gain; and I always thought this kind of furniture was-
artistic. But I think that when Bob saw it he was-well,
disappointed. I wish you'd tell me honestly (it won't hurt
my feelings) is is it very bad?"
They had reached the dining room now, and Helen's heart,
which had been slowly sinking at the ordeal before her, sud-
denly lifted. Mission! Dark wood, unvarnished. Cheap, but
plain, thank heaven! And it might have been golden oak
and curlicues, and shiny. She answered in real relief: "It
isn't bad at all. Bob probably compared it in his mind's eye
with the old mahogany his grandfather used to have here.
And you've some beautiful silver. More wedding gifts?"
But for the moment Bob's "Sandy" wasn't thinking about
wedding gifts. Her face brightened once more.
"Well, you've made me feel better! But I've learned one
thing just the same: I'll never buy anything for the house
again until Bob's seen it. I had hard work not to cry that
evening. I'd thought it so lovely, and Inez (that's my sister)
and I had had such fun arranging the silver on the sideboard.
I could hardly wait for Bob to get home and see the room;
but right off I knew, somehow, by the way he looked, or
didn't look, that I'd made a mistake. But-" Sandra paused,
18

her color rising as she added: "Perhaps I shouldn't have asked

A vision of the beautiful Duncan Phyfe mahogany which

Mrs. Avery Hale had left most of the furniture behind, pre-
ferring the latest style in everything, "including husbands,"
Avery had once observed in what had seemed to Helen a
pathetically bitter joke, for it was an open secret that life was
not "one grand, sweet song" to the Avery Hales.
"I wonder," she said as she sank down on the old daven-
port, "how many times I've sat right here and toasted my
toes on that brass fender! It's lucky for you that Etta Hale
left so much behind, though I see she took the family por-
traits."
"I think it was Uncle Avery who took those," replied San-

had us come to lunch one day when-when his wife was
out."
Suddenly the embarrassment was in her manner again;

grily; and said, to cover an awkward pause: "Avery's a dear."
"Oh, isn't he?" exclaimed the girl, glowing almost as much
as when Helen praised her kitchen. "He's been so good to us.

19
Honestly, Miss Langdon, he likes to come here-drops in for

A puzzled expression crossed Miss Langdon's face.
"But why, my dear, should you do the sweeping? There
are women to come in by the hour even if you don't want
a maid; and Robert's not a pauper. I'll have to nudge him!"
She meant this jokingly, but the girl protested: "Oh, don't,
please! He fusses enough about it as things are; but I made
Bob promise before I married him that he'd let me do the
work and put by whatever a girl would cost. It was that
or I'd keep my job. I didn't earn a great deal at that candy
store; but living at home I could save most of it, and I want
my brother Joe to go to college. He's bright, Joe is. With
an education he can get anywhere; and he's simply crazy
to be a doctor. Bob said he guessed we could manage Joe's
college expenses when the time came, and a hired girl as
well; but I set out to educate the boy myself as far as pos-
sible, and I want to do it. If Bob paid the bills it wouldn't
be the same to Joe-not so good for him. I want him to
realize that I had to work for it, so he won't waste his time
and get foolish ideas into his head. You see, when Mother
died I promised to look out for Joe (he was only three), and
I always have. He's in high school now."
Doing a bit of mental arithmetic, Helen Langdon decided

that Bob's wife was very young when she made that promise.

There was pride in Sandra's voice, and with good reason.
That bathroom was all a 1900 bathroom should be, and Helen
said: "What an improvement! Not much like the old tin
tub boxed round with walnut! It's beautiful; though I've
always had a sneaking regret for the passing of those tubs,
they were so comfortable to read in while one-soaked!"
The girl laughed merrily as they went into the front bed-
room where stood the maple four-poster that had been there
for many, many years. Said Helen, staring at it: "That stun-
ning bed! Why, Sue and Avery were born there, and their
father before them! However did it escape the clutches of
Etta Hale, even though she does prefer new things to old?"
"Bob says she overlooked it in 'her joyous flight," smiled
Sandra. "But later, when she realized what she'd done and
wanted it, Uncle Avery told her that he'd already deeded the
house and furnishings to Bob. Bob says his aunt was mad
as a wet hen."
"She would have been. I've known Etta Hale since she
was fourteen, my dear. No one can tell me a thing about her.
Well, I'm glad that Robert has the bed; and I see you've got
a lovely dresser to go with it. An antique?"
"No, it's a reproduction, and Bob won't tell me what he
paid for it. It was my birthday present, but at the time (don't
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
21
ever let Bob guess it) I was the disappointed one. I've always

There was something so pathetically honest in this admis-

Though it was, obviously, not the work of a trained artist, the
picture, depicting a woodland path, held a sort of charm,
and, watching her, the girl's face brightened.
"My mother did that sketch when she was young, Miss

didn't hang it, so-"

caller noticed, it were a relief to talk about these things:

frightfully." ("Oh, Bob," thought Helen Langdon, "you
must tread with care!") "But when I came in ready for bed,"
Sandra was saying, "he was holding it off, looking at it, and
said: 'Where'd you get this sketch, Sandy? I like it. That
pine tree may be a little out of drawing, but the whole effect
is restful. I'm going to frame the thing.' Do you know, Miss
Langdon," confessed Sandra, shamefacedly, "I was so relieved
I-I almost cried!"
To tell the truth, Helen Langdon, who had a habit of "put-

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
ting herself in the other fellow's shoes," could have done

"Aunt Helen! Gee, but it's good to have you back!" He
was across the room in what appeared a single stride, his
arms about her, his warm young lips on hers. Somehow,
thought Helen, the very feel of those arms said things that
Bob's lips couldn't say-just then. They seemed to whisper:
"Be good to her-please-for my sake. She's been hurt,"
though what the young man really said as he turned to
Sandra who stood there watching them with shining eyes,
was: "Isn't she great, Sandy? Didn't I tell you that she'd
come to see us right away? Isn't she just as lovely as I said
she was?"

"YOU'RE not telling me," gasped Mrs. King, "that-that you
dined at Robert's the very day you called there?"

ment in Susan's voice. The two women were strolling lei-

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL

phone been connected, a delay which, she realized thank-

"Well!" exclaimed Susan, as if more adequate expressions
failed her. Then: "I hardly see, Helen, how you could do it.
Oh, I've been to the house several times, of course. For the
sake of appearances I could do no less; but not once have
I so much as glanced toward the dining room since James
told me she'd furnished it in Mission-and a cheap variety at
that. How Robert can eat his meals on a Mission table-well,
it's enough to ruin the poor boy's appetite, brought up as he
was."
"I saw nothing the matter with Bob's appetite," Helen as-
sured her; "and there are lots worse things, Sue, than Mis-
sion furniture. That black walnut monstrosity in your own
guest room is one example, if you'll pardon my frankness."
"Oh, I'm used to frankness from you, my dear," replied
her friend with resignation. "But, as you know perfectly
well, Helen, Father King gave us that walnut set when we
were married, and Henry wasn't willing to discard it when
styles changed. Besides, in 1869 it was the last word in
fashionable furniture; and almost everyone of our age has
such bedroom sets, if only in attics."
"I haven't," Helen asserted briskly.
"You might have, my dear girl, if you'd had a husband to
contend with," retorted Susan. "Henry was the best and
most attractive man I ever knew, but he wanted his own way
in household matters, as most men do. I sometimes think
the only male creature of my acquaintance who doesn't get
it, is poor Avery; and there are times when I long to shake
my brother for his-his spinelessness."
There followed a silence before Helen answered: "Perhaps
if you and I had spent our lives with Etta Dodge, we'd have
grown what you call 'spineless,' too. But I think you're mis-
taken about Avery, Sue. I believe he's discovered that life

is unbearable without peace, and that the only way to get it

Helen laughed, and as they mounted the steps leading to

The clock was striking five when Stephen King arrived at
Marlborough Street next afternoon. He found Miss Lang-
don looking very handsome in a Paris dinner gown, and told
her so as they sat down before the fire.
"It's only my fine feathers, Steve," she laughed, touching

"Yes, but-" Stephen hesitated, "but your life's been on the

have a chance to broach the subject of a tea. Good-night,
my dear."
IV
On a morning late in the next week when Sandra stood in
her sunny kitchen washing breakfast dishes, she turned at
the sound of stealthy footsteps to behold what seemed to be
merely a head crowned by a black derby, peering cautiously
around the outside door.
"Hi, there!" whispered the head as it caught her eye, “is-
is he gone?"
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
33
Sandy laughed.
"If you mean Bob, he's been gone for half an hour. Do
come in, Father. No one's going to eat you. How's every-
thing at home?"
"So-so."
The head emerged, propelled by a stocky, thick-set body;
while Sandra, drying her hands, went forward to greet her
parent with a kiss. Mr. Bowen beamed, sat down on the
chair she indicated, and pushed the derby far back on his
bald head.
"I had to run in town for some supplies," he explained as
his daughter returned to the dish pan. "Thought I'd stop
by and see how you was gettin' on. But I wasn't sure he'd
be gone this early in the morning."
"And how long," asked Sandra, her eyes severe, "are you
going to continue arriving at your own daughter's house in
the manner of a sneak thief? And what makes you so terri-
fied of poor Bob? Has he done anything he shouldn't?"
"Oh, no, my dear!" denied Mr. Bowen quickly. "It's not
what Bob does, but what he is, maybe, that makes me drop
in when he's likely to be off. If you weren't married to him,
Sandy, I've a notion I'd like the chap real well; but bein'
one o' the family, and so-so different-well...”
Mr. Bowen seemed incapable of finishing this explanation,
and Sandy scolded: "I'd like to shake you! You're just plain
silly. Why even Joe isn't one bit afraid of Bob!"
"Maybe not, my dear, but the kid's different too-more

V
WHEN Robert King reached home that night he found his
wife contemplating the dinner table, so intent on her inspec-
tion that she had not heard his key grate in the lock. Thus
when he greeted her with a cheerful "Hello, there!" Sandy
uttered a startled exclamation, and coming close Bob took
her in his arms.
"Gee! did I scare you?" His voice was contrite. "I didn't
suppose the setting of a table was so absorbing-that is, unless
some august company was expected, which I hope to thunder
is not the case to-night."
"Don't you like company?" questioned Sandra, slipping
from his embrace to straighten a teaspoon. She smiled up at
him, and Robert kissed her again before replying:
"Not so much as I like solitude à deux, my dear. Do I
smell roast beef? And are there roasted potatoes and York-
shire puddings? I love you, Sandy."
The girl laughed happily. She hadn't an idea what Bob
meant by "solitude à deux," but it was evident that he liked
to be alone with her.
"Do you love me just because I make good Yorkshire pud-
dings?" she asked him, her eyes scanning the table once
again. "And speaking of company, Bob, I've already had my
share to-day. Father dropped in before I'd done the break-
fast dishes; Miss Langdon was here to dinner-I mean lunch
-and Uncle Avery arrived just in time to have dessert with
us. I was terribly glad I'd made ice cream."
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
39
"I like my wife having gentlemen callers when I'm away,"
Bob grumbled, while his eyes smiled at her. "Is dinner
ready? I'm a ravening wolf, darling. Had only crackers and
milk at Thompson's Spa this noon."
"It'll be on in a jiffy," promised Sandra, lifting her eyes
from that intent perusal of the table. Then, flushing as she
always did when a bit embarrassed: "Bob dear, why didn't
you tell me that I've been putting the napkins on the wrong
side?"
"Have you?" queried Bob blandly, having no least inten-
tion of explaining that he hadn't mentioned the simple fact
because it was relatively unimportant in his eyes, and he felt
that his wife had stood criticism enough from others without
his joining the procession. But Sandy was not to be deceived.
"You know I have. I ought to have noticed when we dined
at your mother's; but I was too paralyzed with fear that night
to notice anything. And it was almost as bad the time we
went to Stephen's. I was scared blue. But I did have my
eyes open at Miss Langdon's on Sunday, and-"
"For Pete's sake!" exploded Robert, "why should you worry
about that? Who'd notice if-"
"But folks do notice such things, Bob-women especially;
and the napkins were wrong when Steve and Laura came to
dinner. Don't you see, dear, I'd rather have you tell me
things like that than to go on making awful social errors."
Though her face showed genuine distress, Bob had to laugh.
"Social errors! What do you take me for? A book on
etiquette?" Then as she didn't smile he gave the girl a little
shake and kissed her once again. "Don't, please, make your-
self unhappy over such trifles. And if you could feel the
awful vacuum I've got inside, and realize what torment the
aroma of that roast beef is causing, you wouldn't keep me
waiting for my dinner another minute."
This was real tact on Robert's part, and his wife smiled.

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
"Poor dear! Sit down. I'll bring it in."

No, the girl decided, catching back those words abruptly,

45
"See here," Bob interrupted. "If an invitation would give

For just a moment Robert King regarded his young wife
in silence. He knew instinctively that while Sandra was in-
tensely loyal to her own people, she was as alive to Inez'
shortcomings as he was himself. But that she should feel
it necessary to apologize for inviting her sister to a family
dinner, rather appalled him. What had he done, Bob asked
himself, to make her feel like that? He said, gently: "Why
in the world should I mind, dear? And if I did, you ought
to do it just the same. You've put up with a good deal from
-from my family, Sandy. I sometimes think that yours must
have a pretty poor opinion of us, and What makes you
smile that way?" he broke off suddenly.
Her smile widened as the girl confessed: "I was remember-
ing Father. He's so worried for fear I'll regret the day you
saw that stick licorice in the shop window. He says that if
ever you show signs of of wishing you hadn't, he'll wipe up
the floor with you, or something to that effect! I had to
laugh at him, the dear was so worked up."
But Robert didn't laugh. He answered soberly as he arose
to carry out the platter: "If ever I do anything to hurt you,
Sandy, I hope he does."

VI
Ar the Bowen supper table that evening the atmosphere was
charged with the displeasure of Inez.
"What's eating you?" questioned Tom Bowen, Junior, as
she thumped a plate down in front of him with unnecessary
clatter; and when his sister refrained from answering, young
Joe explained:
"She's mad because Sandy wouldn't get her invited to that
party-the one Bob's sister-in-law is giving specially for her.
Didn't Dad show you the clipping from the paper?"
"Oh, that!" Tom's voice was scornful. "What makes you
want to mix up with those swells, sis? I wouldn't go to that
party for a thousand dollars-and that's flat."
"Indeed?" responded Inez with sarcasm, pouring a cup of
tea for her father and slopping half of it into the saucer.
"Well, nobody's going to miss you, anyway. You can bank
on that. But for my own sister to be ashamed of me"
"You shut up!" broke in Tom quickly. "Sandy knows her
own business. She's not ashamed of you, kid; but you can't
expect to butt in everywhere she goes, now she's married
into the the upper classes."
He grinned, while Inez threw him a contemptuous glance.
"Upper classes! I guess I'm just as good as any of those
Kings."
"No, you ain't-aren't, I mean." This was from Joe. "Bob
went to Harvard College, and so did his father and his grand-
father. Sandy told me so. They're educated and refined and
everything, and awful rich."
"And Sandy doing her own housework," shrugged Inez,
still sarcastic. "Stingy, I'd call 'em. When I get hitched it
won't be to any guy that wants a cook."

47
"It better not be!" retorted Tom; while Mr. Bowen broke

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
or get stuck out in the country and not get back all night.

49
"I guess she can wait till we get the dishes done," Tom re-

Tom grinned.

"I've heard that fine feathers make fine birds, but I never

VIII
On an afternoon late in January, Mrs. Robert King left a
trolley car at a familiar corner in Roxbury, and in a few
moments was at the back door of her father's house. She
was wearing the fur-lined coat which was Bob's Christmas
gift, and as she stooped to unlock the door, a woman, who
had been staring at her from a window beyond a high board
fence, suddenly opened it and called: "That you, Sandy
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
59
Bowen? I didn't know you for a minute, you look so swell!"
Sandy turned, waving her muff by way of greeting.
"It's me, Mrs. Sullivan. We got back from Florida last
night. I thought I'd come over and surprise the folks by
getting supper for them."
"I bet they'll be glad to see you," replied the neighbor.
"Inez don't take natural to housework, seems to me. I see
you got a new coat, Sandy. It fits real good, but you must
have paid a pretty penny for it. Them fur collars adds a lot
to the price. Did you get it at a sale? Goodness! I can't
stand here coolin' off the house a minute longer. So long."
She slammed down the window and vanished, while
Sandra stepped into the kitchen where she had spent so many
hours in days past, noticing with disapproval that the break-
fast dishes lay unwashed in the sink, and that small rolls of
dust had congregated, sociably, as it were, under the table.
Mrs. Sullivan was no doubt right. Inez didn't "take natural"
to housework.
"But it's a shame to leave things this way," scolded her
sister, though there was nothing but the empty house to profit
by her indignation. "I'll have to slip into something else and
do some cleaning. It's lucky I came early."
She removed her coat, hung it carefully on a hook in the
front hall, and went upstairs in search of more suitable
raiment for scrubbing. The beds were made, but evidently
in a hurry, especially her father's on which the pillows ap-
peared to have been merely thrown, while his flannel night-
shirt sprawled across a chair where he had left it when dress-
ing. From force of habit Sandra hung it neatly in the closet
and straightened the pillows before turning to the room she
had once shared with Inez.
Here, at first glance, things looked more orderly, and a
bright pink counterpane adorned the bed. The bureau, how-
ever, was in the usual confusion, containing, among other
60
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
odds and ends, a banana peel well sprinkled with talcum

"Who in the world-" she murmured, and, lifting the

a day under twenty-six. "I never saw him before," observed
Sandy, puzzled, "yet there's something sort of familiar about
his face. I wonder..."
She sat down on the bed and slipped the picture from its
frame, to find the name of an exclusive Back Bay photog-
rapher indented chastely on the mount; and, turning it over,
saw written in a slanting scrawl: "For Inez, the cutest little
nail polisher in Boston, from an ardent admirer."
Slowly, Sandra put back the photograph, laying it down
again by the banana skin. "Some fellow whose nails Inez
takes care of," she reflected, troubled. "We shouldn't have
let her do that sort of work, but I never dreamed she'd get
to know those men; and this one's good-looking enough to
turn almost anybody's head. And Inez just at the silly
age..."
...
Her heart sank as she slipped out of her wool dress and

closet. She'd have to talk to Inez, Sandy decided; but just
now when she'd been away almost a month, she hated to

had had dinner at West Cedar Street. They'd set some day
next week. That would be better. But really, Inez ought to
be willing to take good care of Father. He was so patient,
but he hated things all in a mess like this.

It was on the following Friday that Sandra received a call
from her father. As usual, he arrived via the kitchen door,
but she insisted this time that he come upstairs into the
"back parlor," where he sat down uneasily before the fire.
The intervening days had been something of a nightmare
to Sandra King. In the chill dawn following her "family"
dinner party, she had made up her mind to see Inez with-

Sandra King never knew how long she stayed in that tea

Hale.

think about me, Uncle Avery. We both accepted that long,
long ago. But for those who know him to accuse Bob of
anything so dastardly, even though they blame me for it-
Oh, don't you see? I've brought him all this trouble, and it
breaks my heart."
Another silence. Then Sandra said, her voice trembling
a little: "The world is very cruel, Uncle Avery."
He turned, throwing her a wistful smile.
"I wish you need not have found it out so soon, my dear."
"Then it has hurt you, too?"
Avery nodded, not meeting her eyes now.
"It hurts most of us, I fear, if we live long enough. Did
you really expect Robert to be exempt? He made a hasty-
an impulsive marriage, Sandy, and there are always evil-
minded people to pounce on such a happening and make the
most of it. Bob should have thought of that before he mar-
ried you, and gone more slowly. He should have considered
what you'd have to face. That is the one thing I blame him
for."
"Then you should blame me too," she answered sadly.
"Perhaps I should have foreseen all this myself. But Bob's
mother was making things so difficult. He said it was the
only way, and that once she got used to the idea- Uncle
Avery," the girl broke off, distressed again, "do James and
Stephen think-"
"No!" interrupted Avery Hale. "Dear child, don't torture
82
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
yourself unnecessarily. Even Jim, who's never been so close

idea. You mustn't grieve too deeply over this, nor feel dis-

She looked up, a tremulous smile curving her mouth.
"I'm afraid that I do, but mostly on Bob's account, of

He nodded, looking into space.
"Long years ago I used to come here with a girl I loved,
Sandy-only I didn't realize then how much I loved her!
Come!" The man started up as if already regretting this
admission. "It's growing late. We must get on."
"But-do I look too dreadful?"
She raised her face, while with kindly tact Avery ignored
all the disfiguring tear stains.
"You never look any way you shouldn't, Sandy. We'll
cross the Common. A walk in this brisk wind will sweep
the cobwebs out of your brain. I wish"-his anxious eyes
met hers-"I wish, my dear, you'd promise not to worry."
"How could I?" answered the girl with honesty. "But
I'll try, Uncle Avery, if that makes you happier."
Thus it was warmed, and even subtly comforted that San-
dra went out into the early winter twilight. For a little space
her own unhappiness had obliterated from her mind all
thought of the danger that was menacing her sister.

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
"There's nothing meaner," agreed Bob, "and if some fellow

His eyes met hers so shrewdly, so piercingly, that Sandra
arose on the pretext of bringing him some hot potatoes.
"Nothing more now," she answered. "Why should there
be?" But Robert King, who understood every inflection of
his wife's voice, knew at once that she hadn't spoken the
whole truth.
XV
SANDRA KING lost five pounds in the weeks that followed.
Deception was something new to her, and she could not
make up her mind to tell Robert her fears about Inez and
his cousin, thus bringing him fresh cause for worry. Nor
would she confide in her father. She knew only too well
what Thomas Bowen would do in such a situation. Though
possessing the kindliest of natures, the man appeared relent-
less when roused to anger, and was so strong that when his
children were small he refused to chastise them, fearing to
hurt more than he intended. Sandra shuddered to think how
Lester Hale's handsome countenance might suffer if her
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
97
father suspected the young man of playing fast and loose
with Inez. And "playing," she felt sure, was putting things
mildly. Not for one minute had Sandy believed that excuse
about the punctured tires. It surprised her that Inez should
have invented such a flimsy tale. And suppose it were true?
Suppose they had been stranded some twenty miles from
home-Lester Hale was no Sir Galahad. Hadn't Bob told
her that his cousin had only to glance at a girl and
...
A week slipped by before Sandra could force herself to
make an effort to see her sister. Inez had wounded her so
deeply that forgiveness seemed, just then, impossible. It
took that week of keen unhappiness to make her realize that
the younger girl had used that piece of slander in self-defence:
a futile effort to justify her own actions. This knowledge
eased the hurt a little, though the young wife felt that never
again would she feel quite comfortable with her husband's
friends.
To make things harder, there had been two social engage-
ments for the Robert Kings that week-a card party at

XVII
WHEN Robert King reached home after his stag dinner at the
Harvard Club, he was surprised to find Sandra still up.
"Did Lester take you on a spree?" he questioned, tossing

me to tears, Bob, but Inez loved it. Then I rode out to Rox-
bury with them both, and back here with Lester. We had
dinner at Young's. I gather that your cousin is in funds
just now."
Robert laughed.
"Les always does things right, my dear, or not at all.
How'd he happen to invite you at the last moment?"
Sandra had started up the stairs, Bob following as she re-

appeared out of nowhere and insisted that we dine with him.

Aunt Etta! She was with another woman, and stared at us
just about every minute. Lester said her eyes caused cold
shivers to run up his spine; and they made me feel-well, as
if I were doing something clandestine."
Bob laughed again.
"No doubt she thought you were! Aunt Etta possesses an
evil mind. She won't have Lester in the house, you know;
and wouldn't be found dead with him-at Young's. I dare
say she can't imagine the poor chap doing anything from a
decent motive."

XVIII
"Ar last I understand how a fellow is tempted to commit
murder," said Robert King.
It was late the same afternoon and he was seated in Helen
Langdon's living room, a room so spacious and restful that
it was usually soothing to both mind and body; but Miss
Langdon saw at once that it would take more than atmos-
phere to soothe Bob now. At the first opportunity he had
reverted to childhood's days and taken his trouble to the one
place where he was sure of understanding and wise counsel.
He never knew that after granting his telephoned request for
a little of her time, Helen had canceled reservations on a train
for Chicago, and telegraphed the friend she was to visit that
she must delay a day; for she, too, had received a call from
Mrs. Avery Hale.
As it happened, Robert had not seen Miss Langdon for
several weeks, and she was shocked at the change in his
whole bearing. Bob had always been the most boyish of Sue's
sons-the most irrepressible. Now, thought Helen with
something of a pang, that winning quality was lacking. His
face was harassed, and he scarcely smiled when she came in.
"I don't believe you need to tell me anything, Bob," she
began at once in a desire to make things easy for him. "Your
Aunt Etta stopped here after her call on you. She was-
well-the way she would be!"
It was then that Robert confessed sympathy for the sensa-
tions of a murderer.
And Helen didn't smile. She said: "I know, my dear. I
understand; and if wishes could kill, I fear that Etta Hale
would never have reached middle-age. Yet she has her good
points too, Robert, and perhaps we shouldn't lose sight of
them altogether. I've known Etta to be very generous with
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
113
money; and she's working hard to establish a Home for-"
"Fallen women?" put in Robert with new grimness.
"Listen, Aunt Helen. She had the face to imply that Sandy
and her sister were among the 'fallen.' I ordered her out
of my office-politely, of course, if such a thing can be done
politely. Am I to blame for that?"
Miss Langdon shook her head, more shocked by this revela-
tion than she thought wise to say; and wondering if Bob's
conduct would make things harder for poor Avery.
"What else could you do?" she asked after a silence. "Tell
me, Bob, you knew, didn't you, that Sandra was dining with
Lester Hale that night?"
Robert nodded, so wearily that Helen felt a wave of pity
sweep over her. Youth, she told herself, was the time for
happiness; and the years go so fast! It wasn't right that
Bob and Sandra should be so harassed.
"Yes, I knew," Robert was saying. "Sandy tried to tele-
phone (I was home dressing for a stag dinner), but our line
was in trouble so she sent a hasty note by a messenger. She
was afraid I might worry if she wasn't there before I had to
leave. Later she told me that they met Lester by chance and
he insisted they dine with him-said he owed Sandy a dinner,
which was true enough. It was just bad luck that Aunt Etta
happened to be at Young's that night; but- Damn it!"
burst out the young man suddenly, "I'm tired of being picked
on! Why can't they leave us alone? There's no peace in the
world!"
His lips were drawn into a too-straight line as he looked
up and met her eyes, and Helen knew that a storm of tears

want the facts."
XIX
TEN days slipped by and Sandra had not succeeded in having
another talk with Inez. During the momentous dinner with
Lester Hale their differences had been decently ignored; and
the younger girl had no intention of allowing her sister to
revive the unwelcome subject. Nor was her conscience quite
comfortable when she recalled the stricken look in Sandra's
eyes that day in the tea room; though of course, she told
herself, Sandy was a simpleton not to have known what
people would say about her hasty marriage to a fellow like
Bob King. What was the use of being so darned innocent?
Thus Inez took to leaving the barber shop by a back
entrance which opened on an alley, and was "busy" whenever
summoned to the telephone; while Sandra, desperate at her
failure to corner the girl away from home, went out to Rox-
bury for supper one evening when Bob had to work, only to
find that Inez would not be in till late; nor had she returned
when Robert came for his wife at ten o'clock.

his wife by taking her close to a light in order to scan her

all, the fate of her sister was vitally important to them both.

ROBERT KING did not go straight to his office next morning.
Instead he went uptown to Lester's place of business, de-

"I'll call you up as soon as I get a chance," he promised a

Although this supposition seemed reasonable enough, Bob

Bay station. In the meantime, you'd better make another

Sandra hung up the receiver, closing her eyes a moment.
She had waked with a headache-a fact which she had kept
from Bob-and now her throat was beginning to feel sore.
A great weariness seemed to be creeping through all her
bones. She felt physically unable to battle with her sister-
even to battle with life as it was in these days; and suddenly
a great longing for her dead mother engulfed the girl-a
dreadful homesickness.
"If only I could talk with her!" she said aloud. "If only
I could ask her what to do! I can't tell Father; and I'm so
tired of keeping things all bottled up inside me. And Bob's
worried enough as it is."

the time would ever come when they'd do things for her

XXIV
Ar half past four on that same afternoon, Inez Bowen
mounted the softly-carpeted stairs at West Cedar Street, and
entered her sister's bedroom for the first time since Sandra's
illness.
"I'll take this chance to run out for a breath of air," ob-

an errand to do at Hovey's."
"I've got all the time there is," responded Inez. "You
don't need to hurry," and added, after hearing the front door
close behind the nurse, "That's true, sis. I suppose you might
as well hear the worst. I've lost my job."
Sandy, who was back in bed and had been reading "David

She felt suddenly weak as she exclaimed: "You have! What
was the matter?"
Inez flushed, though she tossed her pretty head defiantly.

Her sister's brows drew together in a puzzled frown.
"What do you mean?"

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
153

On that day when the story of the King robbery broke in
the Boston papers, Helen Langdon did not wait for a sum-
mons from her old friend. An early riser, she reached the
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
157
Beacon Street house while Susan was still loitering over a

"MRS. HENRY KING ROBBED BY HER OWN
NEPHEW" occupied an important position on the front

"Well, you're 'news' now, Sue," Helen responded with a

afraid to step foot outside the house for fear one of those

call it."

"That's him," said Inez. "Wasn't he the sneak to let me
down? All that money, and me in such a hole, and he just
skipped."
"Did you meet him here?" questioned the nurse, her eyes
thoughtful as she realized all the answer would mean to
Sandra King.
"Not the first time. I fixed his nails in the barber shop
where I was manicure. I told him I was coming here to
supper one night last winter, and he worked Bob for an
invitation. But we'd been off before. My father thought
I was staying with a girl friend. He's sort of innocent, Dad
is; but-God! he'll kill me if he finds out! I-I gotter get
rid of it, Miss Howland, before he knows; and that doctor
Les sent me to is in prison."
"That sort's likely to land there sooner or later," was the
dry response. "How'd you find out about him?"
"I went to the address Lester gave me, of course. It was
the very day I landed here. That's why I was all in and-
and scared too. The woman at that place insulted me. She
said the doctor'd been sent up for twenty years and good
riddance to bad rubbish; and she'd thank me and my kind
to keep away from her house. She shut the door in my face.
I didn't know what to do, Miss Howland. I was shaking
all over and sick to my stomach, too. Pretty soon I wan-
dered into the next street and sat down in a little sort of
park that ran through the middle. I guess that was a nice
street once."
Inez paused a moment for breath, and then went on:
"After a while I noticed a doctor's sign across the way, so
I went there. He was a good guy, but he wouldn't do a
thing for me said no respectable doctor would touch a case
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
163
like that, though I offered him the whole two hundred. He
gave me the address of some place where he said they'd keep
me and get the baby adopted afterward said they'd be kind
and not ask too many questions; but-but I won't have a
baby, Miss Howland," broke out Inez passionately, "I won't!
I'll jump into the river first. I don't see myself sitting around
for months and months with all those girls in the same fix
I am, just waiting. I'd go out of my mind."
Miss Howland nodded, having had this thought herself
when visiting such places.
"And I won't tell Sandy," Inez was saying. "She wouldn't
understand-not in a million years. Sandy's old-fashioned,
and awful strict. Why, once I told her that folks thought
Bob married her because he had to (Lester Hale told me
so), and well, I thought she was going to pass out right in
front of me. Her eyes looked terrible."
"I should think so!" Miss Howland's voice sounded so
indignant that Inez looked up in some surprise. “A wonder-
ful woman like your sister-and such a man as Mr. King!
The idea of you repeating that story! But you needn't be
so sure she'll blame you, even if she can't quite understand.
And you'll have to tell her, you poor kid. There's no other
way."
"Then you tell her," begged Inez wretchedly; and added,
her young voice bitter: "but maybe I'd better shoot myself
instead."
"The folks that talk that way are the ones that never do
it," observed the nurse. "I'll see that your sister knows, Miss
Bowen, if that'll help any; and now I'm going to get you a
nice lunch. You didn't touch your breakfast."
But it was Robert who told his wife the truth. She looked
at him in silence for a moment, her eyes stricken.
"My sister!" she said after a while. "My little sister that
164
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
I used to think was as beautiful as a fairy! Oh, how I've
prayed and prayed it wasn't so! What can we do, Bob, to
make it easier for her? I feel, somehow, as if it were my
fault."
"It was not your fault, dear," Robert told her, his voice
gentle, "but we'll do everything we can, of course. She can
stay right here with us, Sandy, if that's what you want."
And at his generous offer, the realization of how her sis-
ter's plight might affect Bob's life, and hers, swept over
Sandra King in a revealing flood. What would his people
say, she asked herself those impeccable Kings who trod the
narrow way so easily? What could they say? It was a long,
long moment before her voice would come, and then the
words trembled as Sandra answered:
"Not that, dear. It's like you to suggest it, Bob-I might
have known you would; but there must be some other way,
and-and I shall find it."
The nurse was right, thought Robert, looking down
proudly upon his wife: Sandy would take this "standing up."

IF during the days that followed, Susan King wondered why
her brother's wife had not appeared to say, at the very
least, "I told you so!", her friend Miss Langdon was not
surprised at this extraordinary omission on the part of Etta.
She was aware that Avery Hale possessed a weapon which
might have silenced his wife's tongue, having, quite unin-
tentionally, put it into his hands herself.
This occurred on the night following her return to Marl-
borough Street after the stay with Sandra. Avery had
dropped in, presumably to ask how things were at Robert's,
but in reality because he was feeling fagged and discouraged,
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
165
and knew that an hour spent in Helen's cheering presence

As it happened, Miss Langdon was about to start down

deftly up a sleeve, and said with a welcoming smile: "Why,
Avery! How good of you to save me from a dull evening!"
His eyes brightened, all depression vanishing as if her very
voice held magic, as indeed it did-for him.
"Then you're not going out?" He had noted the evening
wrap which Helen threw carelessly across a chair.
"Of course not. Just make yourself at home while I give
Maggie an order about breakfast. There's a letter from Ted
Mason-youngest son of your old pal, Tom, you know, right
on my desk. It may amuse you. I ran into the boy in Paris
last summer and gave him some letters of introduction which
he appreciated."
She was away, not for any housekeeping orders, but to
send her theatre ticket to a neighbor; while Avery crossed the

Governor of Massachusetts, Helen's uncle. There was no

Its very familiarity caused the man to take it up, regarding

XXVII
It was Miss Howland who solved the difficulty which faced
the household on West Cedar Street. On the day following
her talk with Inez she came into Sandra's room, closed the
door, and said quietly:
"I know what's worrying you, Mrs. King; and I've got an
idea that may help out. You're bothered about what's best
to do for your sister. She can't go home, not with all the
neighbors lifting their eyebrows as time goes on, and gos-
siping the way folks do. It wouldn't be fair to your kid
brother, for one thing; and it would about kill your father.
As for an operation, illegal or not, it's too late now to inter-
fere. I asked her about everything last night; and that baby's
coming in September, or I miss my guess. She's got to go
through with it, Mrs. King, and afterward get the child
adopted."
The nurse, who had been talking rapidly, paused for
breath; and Sandra said: "Oh, why couldn't it have been
me going to have a baby-instead of Inez? I'd love one so."
"Well," responded Miss Howland, "that's Life, I suppose.
I never could understand what they call 'the ways of Provi-
dence.' Now you listen. There's no use sending a high-
strung girl like Miss Bowen to one of those homes with a
capital H. She'd never stick it out six months-never in
this world. But I've got a scheme. I come from Holliston,
Mrs. King. My mother's a practical nurse. She lives a little
way out of the village and would be glad to board your sister
for a reasonable sum; and I'll take my vacation so's to be
there when the time comes and help her out. If neighbors

her form trembled with a convulsive, reminiscent shudder

said, when he could speak calmly: "Look here, Inez, that's
no way to do. You got a tough time before you, but once
it's over you'll forget about it, and life'll seem worth livin'
again, my dear. You're young, child, and they's good times
ahead for you, I'm sure."
"Not," confessed Inez, her lips trembling, "not without
him, Dad."
For a moment Mr. Bowen was too astonished to reply. It
had not, indeed, occurred to any of them that Inez could be
grieving for the man who had wronged her so cruelly-Inez,
who seemed to care so little for her own people! Her father
had to ponder this a moment before he said: "Why, child,
he's not worth the end of your little finger! He wa'n't even
true to the girl he married, Bob tells me; not to mention the
others like yourself. He's a thief as well, and-”
"I know," she broke in quickly. "You don't have to say it.
But he made me love him, Father, and and now, somehow,
I can't stop."
Her mouth worked pitifully. Mr. Bowen could not bear to
look at it. He pressed her bright head down against his
shoulder, and said huskily: "Promise me, baby, you won't
try nothing like what you did this afternoon again. You
promise, dearie, or I can't sleep nights, worryin."
He was stroking her hair as gently as a mother, and, sud-
denly, the girl burst into wild weeping.
"I-promise," she told him between sobs. "I'll see it
through, Dad."
And next day, wearing a jaunty hat adorned with a red
feather, and a collarette which had been the price of sin, Inez
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
179
Bowen departed for her exile, a hard, bright smile on her
childish lips as she said good-bye.

"DID you ever hear of anything more outrageous? Answer

The month was October, and as Helen Langdon met her
old friend's eyes, the thought came to her that it was almost
a year since that day when they sat together in this very
room, discussing Robert King's "impossible marriage," and
that, so far as she could see, Susan was no more reconciled
than she had been then-was, in fact, ready to pounce on any
infinitesimal mistakes of Sandra's, although such errors were
so infrequent that she had to hunt for them. As for Robert,
Helen realized with considerable regret that he had ceased
any efforts to bring his wife and mother together. Bob acted,
she mused, as if he no longer cared whether Susan appreciated
Sandy's splendid character or not. This troubled Helen, who
loved both Sandra and Robert's mother.
It sometimes seemed to her that the whole King family had
been on edge for months. There was James, fuming because
justice was so slow in overtaking his cousin and the stolen
bonds (for Lester had vanished, leaving, apparently, no clue
as to his whereabouts); and, though Jim never voiced the
thought except to Dorothy, furious with his mother because
he suspected her of being inwardly relieved that her brother's
son was still at large.
James King slept badly all that spring; and when his wife
and family departed for a summer in the mountains he re-
fused to accompany them even for a few weeks, for fear that
something might turn up in his absence. Instead, he stayed
on alone in the big house that was usually boarded up at this

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
time, and brooded far too much over the lost money with

None of them ever forgot that ride. James King, waked
from a sound sleep by an insistent telephone summons, had

seen it done; and I'm handy at things like this." He stripped
off his overcoat, throwing it across the seat. "You two can
sit in front while I get started. And don't ask questions.
You can't help, and talking will delay us. I'll hurry, Sandy.
I'll be quick as possible."
His voice was reassuring, but as he worked Bob cursed his
clumsiness. Even when the tire was at last in place and he
had pumped air into it for what seemed hours, he was not
sure whether the thing leaked or not. But they must go on,
he told himself, catching a glimpse of Sandra's anxious face.
If he ruined a tire, or the car, he'd make it good; but in any
case they must not delay longer.
He cranked the engine, unsuccessfully at first, but with a
sudden roar it started, and Bob sprang to the seat. They went
on rapidly, faster than any time since leaving Boston; though
to Sandra, more apprehensive with every moment, they
seemed to crawl.
"It's not far now," Bob tried to tell her; but an express
train, rushing past on the track beside them, drowned the
words as it threw a shower of sparks on high and shrieked
a warning at a grade crossing. South Framingham at
last! Under a street light Bob turned his head to smile at
Sandra. The road to Holliston. Never before, she

thought, had it seemed so endless.... And then a white

196
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
how her fate hung in the balance, and was pleading her

Robert's offer came at a moment when they were all tense,
and sad, and sorry for each other? He hadn't had time to
think things over-to see clearly, or to realize all he was
undertaking. You sniff at my convictions about bad blood-
at my feeling that one's heredity really counts but how do
we know what riff-raff that baby comes from? A light
mother, and God knows what sort of father. It's too big a
risk. But if I were sure, beyond question, that the child had
a grain of good inheritance-something to build on-some
background-"
"She has," said Avery. He had come in quietly, unknown
to either of them, and as she glanced up his eyes met Helen's
in a smile that said, "You've done a good job, my dear, but
let me finish it." For Avery Hale had made no promises.
He played the trump card almost proudly. "She has, Susan.
The finest possible of backgrounds. She is Richard's grand-
child!"

bright afternoon in late September of the year 1919,
a distinguished-looking gray-haired man was walking
briskly across Boston Common when he became aware of
hurrying footsteps behind him, and in another moment a
light touch fell upon his arm.
"Hi, there, Uncle Avery! Since when have you ceased to
recognize your own flesh and blood?"
Avery Hale wheeled about, a quick smile lighting his face.
"Hi, there, Teresa!" he echoed. "I didn't see you.
Thought you were in New Hampshire. When did you get

"Last night; and in a practically wrecked condition at

received a telegram from our nervous father actually com-

of gross injustice."
"Injustice! Wasn't it better than being locked in your
room? I doubt if Bob could have trusted an adventurous soul

mighty unpleasant. The hoodlums had a grand and glorious

Ar the first certainty that the United States would "get into
the big scrap," as he expressed it, Tom Bowen, Junior, had
enlisted. The age limit proved no barrier in his case, for
Tom had always looked younger than his years, and, under
the circumstances, felt no compunction about tampering with
facts. Thus after the young man departed for training camp,
Sandra had formed the habit of riding out to Roxbury on
Saturday evenings with Bob and the children, to spend an
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
209
hour or two with Mr. Bowen and Aunt Em; for she realized
keenly how much her father missed this elder son, who was,
perhaps, more like him than any of his children.
The old house was strangely quiet with Tom gone, and
grew still quieter; for in what seemed to them all a cruelly
short time, a service flag bearing two stars, one gold, hung in
the window of that home in Roxbury.
It was a blow between the eyes to Mr. Bowen when his
son's name appeared in the first casualty list printed in the
Boston papers. Through some mischance no notification of
Tom's death had reached them. Thus Robert at West Cedar
Street, and his father-in-law in Roxbury, had read the fatal
news at almost the same moment; and leaving breakfast un-
touched, Bob had driven Sandra to Roxbury to stay with her
father during that hard day.
"Grandpa has shrunk," small Philip declared solemnly the
first time he saw his grandfather after this bereavement; and
Sandra smiled, albeit sadly, at the boy's quaint phrasing of a
fact that she herself had noticed. Mr. Bowen did look
smaller. His step had lost something of its buoyancy. And
always there dwelt with him a dread of what those sad lists
in the paper might reveal a fear that Joe, too, might have
"gone West."
For Joe Bowen was with a medical unit in France. Often
during those anxious days when she recalled the tall young
doctor, so poised, so perfectly at ease, so handsome in his
brand new uniform, who had kissed her good-bye, saying
with queer abruptness: "Buck up, darling, I'll be back" (he
had also kissed Sally King, Jim's daughter, who, learning of
the trip to Camp Devens at the last minute, had begged to

brother whose hands she had scrubbed such countless morn-

cookie in his lunch box. For Joe was a success. Even should

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
he not come back and Sandra refused to face any such pos-

"Young!" thundered a hoary veteran of the profession when

And Joe had grown. With the help of Bob, of Sandra, and

Grandpa. He says why don't we start, and shall Aunt Em

These Saturday evenings were a bright spot in Mr. Bowen's
life. He adored his grandchildren, especially Teresa, who
had in some small measure healed the wound, the many
wounds (now mercifully softened by time and memory) that
Inez had inflicted. During her babyhood he had been tender-
ness itself to this first grandchild, feeling that some inevitable
day she was to be hurt by knowledge from which they could
not shield her. Once, watching the two small girls at play
together, he had said, eyes on Teresa: "I wish she didn't ever
have to know that she's not yours, Sandy my dear."
"But she must, Father, with a sister only a few months
younger than herself. Sometimes I lie awake wondering
how we can do it without hurting her."
But, just as years before he had shouldered the burden of
telling Mr. Bowen of the trouble about Inez, so Bob took
upon himself this other task that Sandra dreaded. It hap-
pened on a day when Aunt Em was sick and she had gone
to Roxbury, taking Helen with her, for Teresa was having
lunch with "Grandma King." Robert reached home to find a
telephone message from his wife: Aunt Em was pretty mis-
erable, and they were staying to get supper for her father.
Would he bring Teresa and come for them right afterward?
The boys were in bed when Robert and Teresa, then aged
eight, went down to dinner. The little girl felt very im-
portant sitting in her mother's place, and pouring after-dinner
coffee out of the silver coffee pot. It was a jolly meal because,
Teresa perceived with inward joy, her father was treating her
exactly as if she were grown up; and it would be fun riding
214
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
to Roxbury alone with him in the evening. Maybe, she

Then out of a clear sky, she said: "Daddy, are Helen and me

"About as near twins as you can be," replied Bob cheer-

"But-but the girls at school say we can't both be eight

Robert thought fast. Never again, he felt, might the
dreaded question arise so naturally; and taking the small girl

understand, in such a way that the operation was performed
almost without pain. There was one tense moment when an
expression of fear crossed the child's face as, grasping the
fact quickly, as Teresa always grasped things, she said: "Then
I'm not your little girl at all, or-or Mummy's?"
Bob held her closer.
"Of course you are! You were a tiny baby whose mother
(and she was Mummy's own sister, too) had died; and we

besides, we knew Helen was coming and would need a sister

Teresa was silent for a moment, but, aware that she was

love me just 'zactly the same, Daddy?"
"You bet we do! Don't you think any more about it,

get your things. Mother and Helen will wonder where we

run off with you somewhere!"
If Sandra King was surprised that evening to have her

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
215
their having ice cream sodas at a drug store though it was

It was over for a long, long time. Teresa never mentioned
the subject until shortly after her fifteenth birthday. She
appeared to have forgotten it. But one afternoon when she

only a queer unsteadiness in her voice betraying any emo-

Sandra was so surprised that for a moment she could not
answer. Then she said wisely: "There are no illegitimate
children, darling. Only illegitimate parents. That's how I
look at it."
"But you know what I mean, Mummy. Was I one? And
how could such a thing happen to your sister?"
So Teresa was growing up. Sandra had hardly realized the
fact before. She answered: "It happened, perhaps, because

"The man," echoed Teresa. She drew a sudden breath.

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
"Who was he, Mother? Who am I, anyway? Tell me, or

Sandra King felt a quick sense of relief. This was safer

And before Sandra could comfort her, or even answer, Bob

"Listen, silly! Of course I don't love you just the way that
I love your sister. I don't love any of my children the same

and the boys theirs. Just ask your mother if it's not so.

home wouldn't be home to us without you? Don't you know
that you're part of us now, just as much part of us and our
life together as the others are? Why should you grieve?"
It was impossible to doubt her father's word. They did
love her. Teresa realized it anew with a sense of comfort.
And what else mattered? But if something did matter, some-
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
217
thing the girl could not quite control, she vowed to keep it
