More than anything, they wanted her to be happy.... Well,
she would be happy then!
And she was. For it had helped immeasurably to know
that in a way she really belonged that she was not only a
Bowen, but a Hale-dear Uncle Avery's honest-to-goodness

had dreaded to tell Helen about Teresa as much as she had

"But she's wondered, of course." Teresa's eyes, the beau-

rather, if you don't mind."
Just when, or how Teresa told her sister, Sandra never
knew, though she became subtly aware that for a while the
two girls seemed even more inseparable than before. Things
appeared to be working out all right in any case; and if both
she and Robert sometimes showed an added tenderness
toward their adopted daughter, the other children were too
free from jealousy-too naturally generous, to be aware of it.
So life went on, and there came war and sorrow and those
Saturday evenings which were a solace to Mr. Bowen's heart.

trouble about Inez, life was working out all right for Mr.
Bowen, too. He was no longer uneasy in Robert's presence.
How could a man be uneasy, he asked himself, with a nice
young chap who called you "Dad"? (He didn't know that

III
THE following Monday was a busy day for Robert King. At
half past three, having a long, important letter to dictate, he
gave orders that he would see no one that afternoon. At
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
219
four o'clock, after an apologetic knock, a young woman from
the outer office entered the room, a sheet of paper in her hand.
"Excuse me, Mr. King, but I didn't know just what to do.
There's a man outside an ex-service man, I think, for he's
terribly scarred and looks so sick that I hated to say he must
come back to-morrow. I did tell him you were in con-
ference; but he asked me to give you this. He had to write
with his left hand."
Impatient at the forbidden interruption, Bob took the paper.
The man was, of course, Teresa's aviator. What a nuisance,
just at this hour when time was precious! Yet it seemed
hardly decent not to see the poor fellow for a minute. Then,
staring down at the awkwardly written words, Bob read:
I bring a message from a man who, when a small boy, used
to drive around the little town of Chelmsford with you in a
red-wheeled goat cart. Important. I'll wait.
"My God!" ejaculated Robert King, at which the girl who
had delivered the note, and his own stenographer, looked up
amazed.
"I'll see him." Robert spoke crisply. "Do what you can
with the first part of that letter, Miss Jenks. The rest must
wait. Show the man in at once, Miss Clark, and on no
account let anybody interrupt us."
There was a brief delay while the secretary gathered up
her papers, but Robert scarcely noticed. He was thinking:
"A message from Lester Hale after all these years! Can you
beat that?" And in another moment he rose to confront the
disfigured aviator whom Teresa had described so vividly-
nor had she overdone it, was her father's verdict. He felt, as
had the girl, that it was almost an insult to look at that
scarred visage; but, despite a quick memory of missing fin-
gers, Bob stretched out a welcoming hand.
220
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
The aviator met it in a left-handed grip. He did not speak,
nor smile, but recalling Teresa's description of what a smile
did to the man's countenance, Bob understood and made an
effort to smile himself.
"You are, I believe, the Canadian aviator who talked with
my daughter on Boston Common last Saturday? She said
you were a classmate of mine, and might drop in. I don't-"
Bob had been on the point of saying, "I don't recognize
you," but realizing that this greeting would be hardly tact-
ful, added quickly: "From your note I gather that you have
a message from my cousin, Lester Hale. Where is he?"
The stranger, who had been standing with his scarred side
toward the room, turned slowly, speaking for the first time;
and at his voice a light of startled incredulity sprang into
Bob King's eyes.
"War has presented him with the perfect alibi," the man
was saying. "The classic Hale profile is no more, Bob," and,
as if utterly exhausted, he sank into a chair by Robert's desk.
"My God!" said Robert King for the second time in fifteen
minutes. He himself sat down, feeling a strange weakness
in his legs and stomach. Not to save his soul could he have
spoken in that illuminating moment; yet curiously, Bob was
not thinking of the stolen bonds, nor of the fact that this
human wreck was Teresa's father. Rather, he was filled
with a sense of blinding rage that for any cause whatever-
liberty, freedom, or even to succor crushed humanity-a civi-
lized world, a so-called Christian world would sanction the
barbarity of modern warfare that could do this.
It was Lester Hale who broke the silence.
"Don't look at me if you'd rather not, Bob," he said quietly.
"Having been a walking scarecrow for some time now, I
quite understand how the sight of me affects people. That
girl of yours! It was admirable the way she controlled her
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
221
feelings. For a little while she made me feel almost-whole
again."
Robert cleared his throat, while with the tact that had
always been what he once called a part of Lester Hale's
"equipment," the aviator went on, asking a question in order
to put him more at ease: "Did you go through the big fight
yourself, Bob?"
Robert shook his head.
"I probably should have if it had continued for a year
longer. Sandy (who says little about it for fear of distress-
ing my mother past all forgiving) believes that war for any
cause is wrong, and was just beginning to be apprehensive
on my account when the Armistice came. They didn't much
want men of our age anyway-preferred to kill off our
younger brothers or our sons. Ted Frothingham went West,
and both Laura's twin brothers. It almost killed her, too.
Incidentally, Jim's son Henry died of flu when in training
camp; and Tom Bowen didn't come back from France. Joe,
who's on the road to being a famous surgeon, did magni-
ficent work patching up-"
Bob stopped abruptly, and, surmising the reason, Lester
laughed, a short, bitter, unnatural laugh as he broke in: "I'd
like to shake hands with Sandy, if they'd left me anything
to shake with. I'm the rankest pacifist that ever came out
of war. Sometimes I wake at night and am nauseated-I
mean that literally, Bob-remembering things I saw, and,
what's considerably worse, things that I did. Yet they called
me a hero! They even gave me the Victoria Cross!"
For a moment Lester's eyes, miraculously untouched above
the livid scars, dropped to his breast and the four medals
pinned beneath his open coat. Indeed, Robert had been
gazing at them fixedly for five minutes, being thankful for
something he could look at without seeming to insult the
222
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
man before him. He was going to speak to ask about the
decorations, when Lester said:
"Well, I didn't come here to waste your time listening to

know that it wasn't right, and that in twenty years or so
most of the survivors and practically all the rest of civiliza-

perhaps when the time comes you'll recall my prophecy.
What I really came for, Bob-" Suddenly the man broke

trying to pick up my trail, I walk into your office absolutely
safe from detection, and so sure of your own decency, Bob,
that I've no fear you'll turn to the telephone and call head-
quarters. It's that, and a question I want to ask, which
brought me here."
There followed a moment's silence before Robert asked:
"What made you do it, Les?"
"A number of things; chiefly, I think, because I'd never
tried to resist temptation of any sort. I was short of cash;
and Inez Bowen was in a blue funk, insisting that I marry
her and go to housekeeping in a two-room flat. Don't look
like that, Bob. If you freeze up I can't go on, and and I

ing else. Though that's not the story. I'd borrowed ahead
on my salary, Bob. It's expensive business running around
with even a girl like Inez Bowen who wouldn't have known
a first rate hotel from a cheap dump, so long as it glittered;
but I happened to be fastidious myself in those days. All
in all, a trip to New York with her had just about cleaned

223
me out (and Inez nagging for a wedding ring!) when your

It was as easy as that! But I'd no mind to be caught with

on the way to New York following a clue. Perhaps any

clumsy yarn, but it was the best I could trump up at short
notice. And I had to work quickly. It was five o'clock
when I left Beacon Street. I took the midnight to New
York; and in the meantime I'd been to South Boston to see
a woman."
"Another woman, Les?"
The tone was so unlike Bob that his cousin winced.
"Another woman," he repeated. "One who, strange to say,
Bob, had cause to think well of me. I'd come to her rescue

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
even seal the package. And when I left I remember saying

He was unbuttoning his shirt, using his left hand awk-

Lester laughed.

with compassion.

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL

...
motely connected with myself. My daughter!" Lester

He swayed a trifle, while Bob sprang up to lay a steady

"Look here, Les. You mustn't go like this. For obvious
reasons I can't take you home with me; but you're ill, and
I must know where you'll be. I'll write, often, so you'll keep
in touch with us. I'll send you Teresa's picture. She'd want
you to have it. And if you need money-"
"Money?" Grim humor twisted the aviator's face. "No,
Bob, old man, I don't need money. I understand that it's not
used where I am going!"
"But I'll see you again?" Robert was genuinely distressed.
"I must, Lester. You sha'n't leave this room until you
promise."
There came a pause while the two men faced each other.
Somewhere above the city's din, a clock struck five.
"All right," said Lester, as one who has surrendered. "I
promise. You'll be seeing me, Bob."
Then, suddenly, as if his wrecked body could endure no
more, the man turned with curious abruptness and strode
230
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
away. But at the door he wheeled about, drawing himself
erect with an heroic effort, and raising his poor maimed
hand, saluted Robert King in soldier fashion.
The door closed softly.

IV
TERESA KING was one of those rare souls who wake with a
desire to get up and begin a brand new day. Whereas her
sister Helen, and even Sandra, welcomed the chance for an
early morning snooze, Teresa declared that she couldn't be-
gin to "live," as she expressed it, too quickly, and was often
dressed before the rest of her family were half awake. If
the morning were fine she would walk a few blocks before
breakfast, in imitation of Miss Langdon, who still kept up
the habit. "And look at her!" boasted the girl when Bob
asked Teresa if she were planning to enter a Marathon,
"Straight as a soldier-slim as a débutante-and sixty-seven
years old! I am the daughter you should have named for
Aunt Helen, Dad, not that lazy bones who's still upstairs
in bed."
On the morning after Robert's call from Lester Hale, Teresa
was down early. Bob also was down, not having slept well,
and thinking he'd follow his elder daughter's example and
walk a block or two himself. He had managed to remark
somewhat casually the night before that her aviator had
called on him that day; and added in anticipation of the
question he knew would follow: "His name was Lester,
Teresa. He was wrong in thinking I wouldn't remember
him. I recalled the man perfectly."
If Sandra's head lifted at the familiar name, Bob chose not
to meet her eyes; and Teresa asked: "Wasn't he the most
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
231
dreadful sight you ever saw, Dad? And how did he look
when he was young-before it happened?"
"He was a very handsome fellow, as I remember him; and
if your mother needs anything further to make her more of
a pacifist than she is now, my dear, one glance at that poor
chap would be enough. Let's talk of something else. I don't
like to think about him."
Later, in the privacy of their bedroom, he told Sandra the
amazing story, ending with: "If you could see Les now,
Sandy, you'd have nothing but pity for him."
"Did he ask to see Teresa again?" At the moment this
seemed far more important to Sandra King than did the
fact that Jim's bonds had been recovered.
"No," answered Bob. "I think he realized it was safer
not to. He might have displayed undue emotion, perhaps,
and roused her suspicions. I've been trying to figure out
some way to help him, Sandy. He says he hasn't long to
live. Did you ever hear anything more fantastic than the
story of those bonds?"
Sandra shook her head.
"Have you told Jim, or your mother?"
"Jim's still at Chocorua-won't be back till to-morrow night.
Dot and the girls are staying on another week. And I wanted
to think a little before telling Mother. It'll be some shock
when she learns of Lester's condition. Gosh! Sandy-I can't
get that awful face out of my mind."
"Don't you think it's rather curious," observed Sandra
thoughtfully, "that he should have suspected about Teresa
when he didn't even know of her existence? Her eyes are
unusual; but I've always thought she looked as much like
you, Bob, as she does like him. The Hale look-you know
what I mean."
Robert King smiled as he replied: "I'm afraid your desire
ran away with the facts, my dear. Teresa has what you
232
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
call the Hale look, of course; but sometimes she resembles
Lester so strongly that it makes me furious. It's as if nature
felt obliged to remind me every now and then that she's
not ours."
"She is ours, Bob."
Bob threw his wife a comprehending glance.
"Of course she is in a certain way. You've been the most
wonderful mother an adopted daughter ever had, Sandy.
I've never seen you show a bit of partiality to Helen."
Sandra smiled, remembering.
"Teresa was so good! Do you recall how she'd wake early
and just lie in her crib talking to herself in her own language,
not bothering us at all?"
"And Helen would yell for the dining car the moment

hungry. You're right, Sandy, Teresa was less trouble than
any of the others. Look here!" His mind went back to the
vexed question. "What can we do to make the rest of
Lester's life a bit more bearable?"
The problem kept Bob from sleep that night. Hence he
arose early, and meeting Teresa in the hall on her way down-
stairs, received a kiss on the tip of one ear by way of
greeting.
"Beat you to the paper, Dad!" she whispered, knowing that
Sandra might still be sleeping, and sped down before him.
War, Bob was thinking as he followed more slowly, had
given these young folks the habit of reading a daily paper.
At Teresa's age the girls of his generation hardly knew what
a newspaper looked like. But his own daughters scanned
the headlines as intelligently as any man. He paused at the
last flight, thinking Teresa might come up into the living

down to find this surmise to be correct. The Boston Herald

The three men were very quiet as they left Mount Auburn
late that September afternoon. Bob wondered if Lester Hale
knew that he was lying beside his father, with Teresa's red
roses above his heart. Life was queer, mused Bob, and ex-
traordinarily hard to understand. How, indeed, could sin,
and shame, and a young man's careless passion bring forth
anything so beautiful so absolutely right as was Teresa? It
was like her to have remembered the flowers.
...
None of them spoke until long after the cemetery gates
were left behind. Avery Hale was thinking of his brother-
wondering why so splendid a man as Richard should have
had a son lost to all sense of honor: poor Lester, who would
harm no earthly thing again.
Stephen was realizing with something of a shock that this
was the first break in his own generation of Hales or Kings.
Who would be next? he asked himself, pushing the thought
back with a little shudder.
But as they moved more quickly toward the city, Bob's
thoughts were still centered on Teresa, whose coming, though
in itself a tragedy, had healed the rift between Sandy and
his mother. Yet they had Uncle Avery to thank for that.
Had he said to his sister, "She is Lester's daughter," not "She
is Richard's grandchild," as he was inspired to do, all these
good years might have been different.
For it was impossible for Susan King not to take to her
heart the granddaughter of the brother she had worshiped.
She had come to see the child one Sunday afternoon when
she knew that Robert would be at home. Sandra herself
laid her sister's baby in Susan's arms, then, with her own kind
tact, left them alone together; and Teresa had done the rest.
"Odd, isn't it," James King once said to his Uncle Avery,
"that the illegitimate child of a cheap little skirt like Inez
Bowen should accomplish what the entire family had failed
to do-make Mother accept Bob's wife as one of us?"

STEPHEN was too late that evening to see the astonishment
on James King's face when Robert handed him the bonds,
still tied with the red twine that had guarded them for
eighteen years. Although Steve had reached home in a more
hopeful frame of mind, his heart eased a bit by the talk with
Robert and his uncle, one glance told him that Laura had
been crying (not that this was anything new these days,
thought Steve discouragedly), and though she smiled as he
kissed her, Stephen knew it was merely a smile forced for
his sake. Poor Laura!
He said: "I want to wash up, darling. I'll be right down,"
and went upstairs more with the feeling that he must be
alone for a moment and shake off the depression these home-
comings always brought, than in a desire for cleanliness. But
in the hall above, Jeannette, one of the twins, waylaid him,
a finger on her lips until they reached the nearest bedroom
where she closed the door.
"Will you be home this evening, Father-no business or
anything to keep you busy?" she questioned.
Stephen nodded.
"What's the trouble, Jeannette?"
His daughter threw him an anxious glance.

At Beacon Street he found the other masculine members
of the family assembled in the "back parlor," with his mother
and Helen Langdon, who had dined there. For Jim's benefit
Robert had just repeated the story of Lester Hale's tragic
end, Avery having already broken the news to Susan. Like
them all, James found the tale almost incredible.
"Do you suppose, Bob, he really told the truth?"
"I know he did. So would you, if you'd talked with him
yourself, Jim. Besides, when a man intends to end every-
thing before another day, he's not likely to lie unnecessarily."
"But where in the world had he been these eighteen years?"
"He wouldn't say, except that, being a fugitive, he'd suf-
fered the pangs of homesickness and found them unbearable.
I couldn't press him with questions when it was evident that
the talk was such an effort. The poor fellow was shot to
pieces. Even his clothes were worn and shabby, especially
the khaki shirt, though there was a row of medals pinned
across it."
"You don't say!" James was obviously impressed. "Did-
did you bury them with him, Bob?"
Robert's eyes closed for a moment. He seemed to hear the
rent of tearing cloth as Lester stripped himself of those badges
of honor.
"No, Jim. He left them with me. Asked me to give them
to-to Teresa-some day."
A silence fell on the Beacon Street parlor. Then Helen
Langdon reached over and touched Bob's hand.
"You've had a lot to go through this last twenty-four
hours, Bob dear," she said; "but I'm glad that Lester left the
medals for Teresa."

Everyone laughed, while James, giving his mother's hand

"I think I'll run along. If you have no escort, Aunt Helen,

James smiled on his uncle. They were exactly of a height,
and looking up at them Helen Langdon thought that Avery
might almost have been taken for Jim's elder brother. Jim
had grown noticeably older since Henry's death; while the
discouraged droop to Avery's shoulders which used to subtly
hurt her, had disappeared. He was a different man since
Etta had stepped out of his life, though the cord that bound
him to her still existed. She had refused to sever it, even at
his request. Yes, mused Helen with a touch of almost bitter
humor, at any cost "Dodge women hold their men."
In the house on West Cedar Street the pop corn party had
been interrupted by the arrival of two athletic-looking swains
displaying tuxedos and a distinctly Harvard accent, at which
young Philip found it difficult to suppress profound disgust.
"You'd never have noticed it was bedtime if they hadn't
butted in," the small boy complained, as, accompanied by
aunt and mother, he dragged reluctantly upstairs. "It's just
my luck. And didn't they look silly all rigged up like that?
I bet when my father was at Harvard he didn't waste any
time calling on girls!"
Remembering Bob King's popularity with the fair sex,
Laura laughed in reply. She had laughed more than once
that evening; but when Sandra returned after tucking her
young son in bed, she found her sister-in-law looking at a

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
picture of Tom Bowen, taken in uniform just before he

At Sandra's step, she turned, laying the framed photograph
on the bureau. There were tears in her eyes, the tears that
seemed to flow so readily in these days. She said, trying to

Sandy? You've lost a brother, too; yet you go right on just
as if life could be the same as as it used to be. Don't you

that you'd rather die than go on living in such a world?"
"Why should I?" answered Sandra, her voice gentle. "I've
got so much, Laura:-Bob, and my girls and boys." She
came nearer and drew the other woman down beside her
on a couch at the foot of the Hale four-poster. "How can
I feel that nothing matters when I know that they all love
me and need me? Yes, I give way sometimes, Laura, when
no one sees."
"You mean you cry, Sandy, when you're alone?"
Sandra smiled a little.
"Not often. When Tom died I was too busy helping
Father through that hard time to think much of what I'd
lost myself. But the day Joe sailed for France, Laura, I was
-all in, as the girls say. I was thirteen when Mother died,
you know, and Joe was almost my own baby. I'd said good-

supposed to know they were leaving-the men themselves

hint. So I knew. It was a Thursday, and the children were
all at school. Nelly had gone for her afternoon off; and I
went downstairs and told Jane she could go too, and not
come back till evening. I was just desperate. Even Bob
didn't suspect how I was feeling. I've never believed that
war was worth the cost, Laura, so I missed the uplift that
helped the rest of you through such times. I kept walking
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
247
around the house, exactly like an animal in a cage; and after
Jane had gone and I was all alone I-I went downstairs and

Laura stared at her, not quite understanding.
"You mean-"
"I mean only that hard work cleared the air. Who was
it said, 'Blessed be drudgery'? I used to laugh at the idea
(there was so much drudgery in my young life, you know),

to heal our frazzled nerves, Laura, so long as our bodies aren't
frazzled, too. That day, after the floor was finished, I set to
work and got a big dinner for the family a hard dinner.
When Bob came home I was tired enough to drop, but I
was myself again. He never knew the turmoil I'd been
through."
Laura was silent, looking down at her hands: delicate,
well-cared-for hands, perfectly manicured. She said after a
while, thoughtfully: "I never scrubbed a floor in my whole
life, Sandy. Steve would think I was crazy."
Sandra smiled.
"And Bob would have been furious-till he understood!
These Kings of ours make splendid husbands, Laura. All
they ask is to take care of us. But I think, perhaps, that for
generations their women have missed something just the
same. Most of them did nothing more strenuous than to
'sit on a cushion and sew a fine seam,' and when sorrows
came-and we all get those, my dear-they couldn't know
what good, hard, honest work would do for them."
Laura's troubled eyes lighted at a memory.
"I know. Once when we had Jim's cottage at Chocorua,
it rained too hard to go to the hotel for dinner, and I cooked
a meal myself. I'd been half sick all the morning or thought
I was; but the cooking was fun after I got started, and I
forgot all about my aches and pains. I suppose you mean"

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
(Laura flushed a little) "that I really haven't enough to do?"
Sandra nodded.
"Don't you see, Laura, it's the terrible let-down. Through

And remembering all those men who laid down their lives
so uselessly, and the others, the worse than dead, who have
come back maimed, you begin to get bitter; and bitterness
can destroy us, Laura, as surely, as stealthily as some dreadful
poisons."
Laura was weeping, but if Sandra noticed she made no

breaking. I lost a little brother once. We didn't have much
money than, and though she was worn out with nursing, my
mother ironed till midnight so we small girls would have
clean dresses for the funeral. When I was older I used to
cry, remembering it; but now I realize that working for the
children she had left, probably helped her. But for women
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
249
like you, with servants, and houses that run like clockwork,

Laura looked up. If her eyes were wet, at least, they were

Sandy. You seem to know so much."
Sandra smiled.
"You ought to be in my shoes for the next two weeks.
Both maids taking vacations; and how I'll manage all those
meals and everything-"
She made a gesture of helplessness so unlike her indomi-

was-good acting; but Laura, intent on her own troubles,
walked into the trap as she asked eagerly: "Couldn't I help
you, Sandy, a little? Would you let me stay here and do

thinks I need a change, and the thought terrifies me. But
if I could come here where you're all so cheerful, and where
I could see Steve every day, I do think I'm almost sure it
would help a lot."
Sandra King suppressed an urge to cheer.
"It would certainly help me!" she answered with enthu-
siasm. "Of course you may come, Laura. We'd all love it;
and Stephen can have dinners here and spend the nights, so
you won't get homesick."
But Laura's eyes grew troubled.
"How could the girls stay all alone?" she asked.

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
Sandra laughed at this.
"The twins? Laura, I could shake you! Why shouldn't

"Why!" exclaimed Robert stupidly, "I didn't know both

"Kids!" This time it was Steve who received a glance of

Bob frowned at his brother.
"That's the second time you've mentioned the unpleasant
subject of approaching age," he said. Then, "Hark! Those

which Stephen seems to think is fleeting all too fast!"
They did, much to the surprise of two young athletes in
tuxedos, one of whom had the audacity to cut in on the
elders, remarking later to a gratified Helen that her mother
"certainly knew how to step out."
Laura's cheeks were pink when she kissed Sandy good-
night. Steve kissed her, too, saying softly into one ear:
"You're a brick, sister. We both love you."
Out under the stars with his wife's arm tucked through
his, Stephen King thought: "Perhaps I needn't bother Aunt
Helen after all."

Already his financial burdens seemed to have lost their

VI
On a morning some five or six days later, Teresa King hung
up the telephone receiver and descended on the kitchen
where her aunt and mother were washing breakfast dishes.
"Good news, menials!" she exclaimed. "You won't have
to get dinner for us girls to-night. We're dining out. Your
daughters are giving a party, Aunt Laura. Fourteen covers
will be laid, and a simply marvelous time is predicted by
all concerned."
Laura was so astonished that she dropped a spotless dish
towel on the floor.
"A dinner party? What do the twins know about order-
ing a dinner? And no chaperon?"
Teresa raised a scornful eyebrow.
"Don't be historic, darling. Aren't you aware that chap-
erons went out with the war? Besides, it's a sort of family
party as to girls. There'll be only the cousins: your kids and
us, and Sally and Marjorie. Then there's let me think-
Uncle Joe (Really, Mother, we'll have to stop saying 'uncle'
if he marries Sally. It would be too absurd.), and Pete and
Leonard, and David Bigelow and a boy who's staying with
him."
"That's twelve," Laura reminded her. "You said fourteen."
"Oh, I forgot! There's to be a chaperon after all, so you
can heave a deep, Victorian sigh of satisfaction. The wildest
of us won't be led astray while Aunt Helen Langdon is on
the job; and the fourteenth cover is for your handsome hus-
band, if he'll honor us. If not, we'll rope in Uncle Avery.
I don't imagine it will take much persuasion on our part,
with his lady love at the other end of the table."
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
253
"Teresa!" reproved her mother. "You should be more care-
ful how you talk. Don't forget that Uncle Avery has a wife

"A wife? I call her a road-hog-blocking the way to hap-

to admit that you've had the same idea yourself hundreds of
times; though I suppose they're too old to think of marrying,
even if Aunt Etta were so considerate as to pass away."
"Neither of them will ever be old," replied Sandra; "and
I don't know what Aunt Laura thinks to hear you say such

But Laura, absorbed in her own problems, had hardly lis-

The girls-"

"That's right, Mother. Give it to her!" laughed Teresa.
"Don't worry, Aunt Laura. Jeannette said the maids were

Teresa smiled.

interested. I told him about the carnations, and he howled.

one Saturday afternoon when we were at Roxbury both Phil

VII
The dinner was over. Now syncopated strains of jazz drifted
in from the big living room at Stephen King's; while in an
alcove across the hall, where they could catch glimpses of the
dancers, Helen Langdon and Steve were talking about Laura.
"I hardly dare mention it, but she does seem remarkably
better when you consider it's less than a week since she went
to Bob's," he admitted. "Every time I even think about the
change, I rap on wood! I can't help fearing that it's only
temporary; and that when the poor girl gets back to these
surroundings, she'll slump again."
"Sandra will keep an eye on her after this, Stephen. You
can count on that."
"I know; but once Laura's home, Aunt Helen, she'll have
time to brood. The girls are busy with their own affairs; and
it's right that they should be. Their mother would be the last
to want them to give up good times for her, but she told
Sandy that she was afraid to be alone. It's true, I suppose,
that Laura hasn't enough to do round here."
He made a gesture that included the whole lovely house
in which the Stephen Kings had lived since just before the
war-an old mansion on lower Commonwealth Avenue, not
markedly beautiful when they bought it, but remodeling and
Laura's unerring taste had made it so. Its purchase had not
seemed an extravagance at that time. Stephen was making
money; and the twins would soon be ready to take their
places in society. "What a dream of a house for entertain-
ing!" Laura had cried when they first considered it. Then
came the war-rising prices-even servants' wages had
doubled-then trebled; and to Stephen King the house where
he had hoped to live out his days, became a burden.
"There would be plenty for Laura to do, Steve," observed
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
259
Miss Langdon, "if it weren't for those three maids in her
kitchen."
"Yes, but how long would she stand up under a job like
that? You know how it is, Aunt Helen. Laura's never had
to work any more than you have, or Mother. She doesn't
know how."
"She could learn. She's learning right now, isn't she?
Laura cooked the entire meal when I lunched at Sandy's yes-
terday; and it was a good meal, too. Avery has told me
about your difficulties, Stephen. If you can't keep up this big
house without worrying, would Laura want you to do it?"
"Probably not; but how can I tell her now, Aunt Helen,
when she's in this unhappy state? What would it do to her?"
"It might cure her, Steve. Didn't Joe Bowen say she
needed rousing? And Doctor Gerry backs him up."
"But it would be absurd to expect her to swing things here,
even with the twins to help. I wish we'd never left the house
on Brimmer Street."
Stephen stared gloomily into space; and after thinking for
a while Miss Langdon asked: "Is the old house still yours,
Steve?"
"Yes; and it's been empty for six months just when I need
the rent! How's that for luck?"
"Not so bad as you appear to think." Helen's face had
brightened. "That's a lovely house, Stephen. It even has

it; but if I were you I'd tell Laura the whole story-ask her

Said Stephen, somewhat surprisingly: "I've thought of that;
but would you expect my wife and daughters to do the

Helen laughed at his obvious dismay, and answered: "Not

people began calling her Madam King, Sue liked it; but it
made me feel like a centenarian to hear them. In ten years,
Steve, she'll be a typical Boston dowager-and like that, too!"
Miss Langdon sighed, and added: "Well, perhaps she's wise.
Sometimes I wonder if I'm one of those silly women who
refuse to accept old age gracefully. What do you think, my
dear? Tell me the truth."
Stephen turned to scrutinize Miss Langdon soberly. Her
figure might have belonged to a girl of twenty; but her hair
was honest gray, beautifully waved, and though her face was
not without the lines that are etched by laughter and by tears,
her skin was soft and her eyes bright. He answered truth-
fully:
"You'll never have to accept old age, Aunt Helen, grace-
fully or otherwise. It can't touch you."
A look of wonder swept across Miss Langdon's face.
"That's strange," she said. "Your uncle told me that very
thing not a week ago!"
Stephen smiled at her.
"Then it must be true, and you needn't worry! What's
that other proposition you mentioned? I'll have to skip as
soon as Uncle Avery comes."
"It's this," she said. "Don't speak, Stephen, until I'm
through. I dare say you know that I'm rather a rich woman.
I don't splurge, but I live comfortably the way I like to live;
yet I don't spend my income. I give away more than anybody
suspects (except Avery, who's always managed my affairs),
262
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
and still I don't have to deny myself the little luxuries I like

"I'm sure it is,” responded Stephen quietly. "And if you're

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
263
to go, and treated her with courtesy and kindness for as many

down there. They were simply great. They had never
allowed frustration nor disappointment to embitter them-

off his tie and collar, that they both knew how much he loved

THE Stephen Kings were going back to Brimmer Street.

Mother often spoke of that with satisfaction."
Sandra snipped off a thread before she raised her eyes.

her way around, and a damned, contemptible cad, who knew

to forgive me. She hasn't-yet."
"Good luck!" answered his niece. She wanted to say
more, but couldn't manage it. Yet that didn't worry her.
Something told Teresa that Joe would understand.

JAMES KING had been away on business for ten days; and the
morning of his return, came into Stephen's office looking
rather weary and decidedly upset. Steve greeted him with
a smile.
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
299
"Hello, Jim! I thought you weren't due back for a couple

"Two damned uncomfortable nights in a sleeper for one

mother, who, perhaps, understood him better than anybody

long as she's the sort of girl you people have made her. I
said a lot, and it did her good. Now leave her alone."
"Don't you think," asked Robert, "that she might feel
better still if I gave her those medals?"
"Not now," said Joe. "That'll come in its own good time,
Bob, but I'd give the wound a chance to heal a bit, if I were
you."
"Well," returned Bob thoughtfully, "I'm certainly grateful
for what you've done, Joe; and I hope it didn't put you out
to go to Roxbury."
"Not at all." Joe threw them a rueful smile. "Sally was
engaged anyhow, when I telephoned from Dad's. It seems
I put my foot in it pretty badly last Saturday."
Robert laughed, remembering the scene.
"It did look, Joe, as if you'd mislaid your customary tact!"
"I must have! I realized it the minute Sandy turned that
fishy stare in my direction. But I didn't plan the thing,
honestly, Bob. I'd been looking at a patient, a boy who'd
gone home from the hospital the day before, and it wasn't
six blocks off. I knew Aunt Em had been feeling mean,
and thought I'd say hello. So I asked Sally to come in. I
supposed you folks would be there, but how in time was I

reader. Of course," Joe admitted, "I see now that I should
have telephoned ahead. In fact, Sally asked me to; but I
didn't count on the house looking so-so-"
"Oh, don't describe it!" broke in Sandra, shuddering. "I
was so ashamed! And Jim's daughter, of all the nieces. If
it had been Jeannette or Stephanie I wouldn't have cared so
much."
Joe began pulling on his coat.
"Sally's no snob, Sandy."
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
309
"I hope not; but think how she's always lived, Joe-and
that red tablecloth!"
Joe's face hardened.
"Well, she'll probably never set eyes on it again." Then,
forcing a grin, "It looks, Sandy my dear, as if I'd have to
go shopping round for a new girl!"
But the grin lasted only until Doctor Bowen reached the
street. To this successful young surgeon with a rosy future
predicted by all his colleagues, the world looked curiously
bleak.
XII
To say that Susan King was gratified at Miss Langdon's suc-
cess in the rôle of ambassador, would be stating the case
mildly. She was elated.
"Of course you may see about the dresses, Helen," she

dear. In all these years I've never forgotten it before. I shall
never forgive myself."
Teresa was stroking the clasped hands gently. She said:
"Tell me about it. Tell me, Grandma dear. Perhaps I can
help."
XIII
THERE followed what seemed to Teresa a long silence before
her grandmother replied: "It is my husband's birthday to-day,
Teresa." She paused in a manner agonizing to the girl who
watched her. "He has been gone for more than thirty years,
my dear, and on his birthdays I've always carried our wed-
ding flowers to the cemetery. White roses and lilies-of-the-
valley, Teresa. He used to send them to me on my own
320
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
birthday, and his; so, after he went, I took them to him.
But to-day, with all the excitement and confusion, it slipped
my mind. I-I forgot entirely. What is he thinking of me,
Teresa, if he knows?"
Her grandmother's question seemed so pitiful to the girl,
her face so harassed, that Teresa had hard work to reply.
Indeed, she wondered what she could say that would bring
help or comfort, until something Robert once told her about
his father flashed like an inspiration into her mind.
"I wouldn't worry about that, Grandma," she answered
cheerfully. "I don't believe Grandfather's feeling one bit bad
about it. I think he's glad you've been having such a good
time that you could forget! And amused at it, too. Didn't
he always see the funny side of things? Dad says he did.
And anyway, Grandma, you remembered in time. You can
take the flowers now."
Susan's face brightened, then clouded swiftly.
"I don't see how I can, child. I'm very tired; and yester-
day when Doctor Gerry dropped in to see me, I promised
him I'd go to bed right after lunch and stay there until
time to dress to-night. Perhaps I've done too much, at my
age, and I must feel well this evening, Teresa, with all those
people coming. Ed Gerry was very firm about it; and usually
he lets me have my way."
"Well then," declared Teresa, "I'll take them for you.
Order the flowers, Grandma, and I'll carry them to Mount
Auburn. Why, look here!" She sprang up, her blue eyes
shining. "There are dozens of roses and bushels of lilies-of-
the-valley right down stairs. I'll make you a bouquet-a
lovely one; and after lunch (I promised Mother to be home)
I'll come right back in the flivver and take them to the
cemetery. I'd love to."
Susan King looked up. Her lips trembled, but Teresa,
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
321
watching closely, saw the lines of distress fade out of her face

Later, when her granddaughter returned with a creation

so stirred up; and that bouquet's the prettiest I ever saw.

it. Now kiss me, you dear child, and run home to lunch.
You're a great comfort."
Teresa was late at West Cedar Street and found her family

Teresa closed her eyes, trying to rest, but her brain was in

say if she knew that her daughter, who ought to be in bed

dear Grandmother! How distressed she had looked. Queer

sentimental, yet here she was just about heartbroken because
her husband, dead for more than thirty years, didn't have
his birthday flowers. "I wonder," said Teresa, half aloud,

The girl would have been amused had she known all

feel old."
He turned, slowing down to an appropriate solemnity of

want to get right back."
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL

you, Dad?"
XIV
LIGHTS shone out from every window of the old King house
on Beacon Hill. From beneath a gay, striped awning which
ran from the wide doorway to the street, one saw a strip of
crimson carpet covering the brick sidewalk and stone steps;
while opposite, leaning against an iron fence which spanned
the Common, a crowd of curious onlookers had gathered,
awaiting the first arrivals and a glimpse of lovely ladies in
lovely gowns.
Although it was still early, some had already come, the first
car driven by a chauffeur in livery with two boys beside him
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
331
who scrambled out unaided, their patent leather pumps shin-

glass of a front window.
"A kid's party?" questioned one onlooker of another, who,
out of long experience in watching similar occasions from
the outside, responded with something of importance: "The
family. Gosh! did you see those gals? They was a sight
for sore eyes, wa'n't they?"
Out of the next car stepped a tall man wearing a silk hat,
a lady whose fur wrap hid her garments, and two young
girls, one of whom, pausing to glance up the strip of carpet,
called, "Hello, Phil!" to an invisible somebody within the
house.
Then, almost at once, the first limousine returned, from
which alighted another tall, slim man, a fragile-looking, ex-
quisite blonde lady, and again two girls, “alike, so fur's I can
see," observed the experienced lounger, settling himself more
comfortably against the fence, "as two peas out o' the same
pod. It's sure lucky for us that street light's handy!" And
after a long wait: "Yes, they was family. I bet we'll stand
here coolin' our heels an hour more."
This prophecy was very nearly fulfilled, for Susan King had
commanded her family to arrive early.
"Goodness knows when I'll have another chance to see you
all dressed up at the same time," she said. "And I want to
gloat over every one of you."
She did, and with good cause.
"These Kings are certainly a distinguished-looking family,"
said Helen Langdon to the gray-haired man who stood be-
side her. "No wonder Sue is proud of them. And isn't Jim
the living image of his father?"
Avery Hale smiled down at her.
"You have the whole King tribe looking like a mob of
peasants, Miss Langdon," he declared.

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL

The orchestra leader, a white-mustached veteran of his
profession who agreed with Susan's sentiments on modern
dancing, watched Mr. and Mrs. Robert King glide across the
floor in the lovely rhythm of pre-war days-made sure that
Miss Langdon and Mr. Hale were there, and decided to have
some fun. These smart young sprigs who stalked about to
syncopation and called it dancing, were going to be shown
something! Most of them couldn't waltz anyway that is,
not in the manner of their parents and grandparents. Put a
little expression into the music, for instance, and they were
completely lost-thought there was something wrong with
the orchestra. Let 'em see!
Thus, facing the two long rooms, this old musician, a
twinkle lighting his blue eyes, directed "Home, Sweet Home"
as when, only a little lad himself, he had heard it sung by
"the Swedish Nightingale"-Jenny Lind, the incomparable.

Susan King threw her a quick smile. "I was wondering,"
she said, "if that idea would occur to anyone!"
"So was I," confessed Helen. "Tell us, Sue, how you
would spend the day if you did just what you wanted."
Susan King closed her eyes a moment, as if considering.
"Do you want me to be honest?" she inquired.
"Of course! What donkeys we all are!" said Bob.
"Well then," began his mother, "I wouldn't have any party
at all-that is, not the sort you're planning. If I did exactly
what I'd like I'd have Helen come the day before and spend

There was a pause; then Stephen asked: "What next,

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
Susan King smiled.
"You'll be surprised, all of you, but I'd spend the whole

A silence fell on the Beacon Street living room. They

"It wouldn't be too much for you, Sandra?"

it to Teresa for her hospital. A stingy King never existed,
so the sum will really help, and that will make me feel that
I've accomplished something by living to be eighty-five."
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
361
Miss Langdon gave her old friend's hand a squeeze.

Bokhara that covered the whole floor. A portrait of Avery's

The three Kings who were strolling down Beacon Hill at

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
had reached him only in time to catch one look of agony

Perhaps none of them realized at the time what a sacrifice
it had been for Helen Langdon to go away just then. She

with Avery Hale that had become the happiest part of her
whole life. Yet the four months had lengthened into six
before she and her charge returned to Boston; and it was
not in Helen's generous heart to regret those months.

Jeannette said to her father when the exile was over, “I
would have been ashamed not to buck up after Aunt Helen
told me what she had gone through when she was a girl

see, I began to do a portrait of Pete, from memory. I never
liked his photographs; and I thought if I could put Pete's
eyes on canvas the way they were when he was happy, I
might forget that agonized last look he gave me. Aunt Helen
cried when she saw what I had done, not only because it was
Pete as we like to think of him, but because she knew I'd
got the better of myself, and she could go home to Uncle
Avery-poor darling. Look here, Dad!"

367
The girl had uncovered a canvas set up on the piano, and

All this was thirteen years ago, Stephen was thinking as

would his daughter be, he wondered, if Helen Langdon, wise
through her own suffering, had not understood the girl's need,
and stretched out a helping hand?
They had reached the parting of their ways, and Robert,

a lot we have to thank Aunt Helen for-all of us, Steve. And
Jeannette's a wonder! I almost expired with pride at her

Laura King laughed.

II
In the house on West Cedar Street there was a bustle of ex-
citement the day that Mrs. Henry King was eighty-five years
old. The earliest sounds of activity emanated from the fourth
floor which was occupied by Teresa and her brood. When
something suggesting a pillow fight began to take place above
his head, Robert stretched and said: "Get another nap, Sandy
-if you can! I'm going to join the scrap," and escaped in
his pajamas to the upper floor.
But Sandra didn't attempt a nap. She lay for a while
thinking over plans for the day and counting on her fingers
everyone who would be there. Teresa and her four, with
Jack if he got down from Vermont in time. He was coming
by automobile and would take his family back with him
next day. Helen, Dave and the three children; Phil (who
was completing a course in Business Administration at Har-
vard in record time, because he was in a hurry to get mar-
ried), and Mary Slocombe, his sweetheart, a dear girl, mused
Sandra, who, as fate willed it, came from Roxbury, only a
rather different portion of the place than that which had
produced Phil's mother long ago!
Counting Bob and herself, that made fifteen right in their
own branch of the family. Sandra sighed, wishing her elder
son might be here, too; though Dick, a civil engineer, was
for the present living in Denver. Even so, he might have
come if his wife hadn't been awaiting the arrival of a second
baby. But Richard Avery King, Junior, was four years old,
and his great-grandmother had never seen him-the only
great-grandchild ("to date," smiled Sandra) to carry on the
name of King. It was a pity that he, of all the grandchildren,
should be absent.
The rest, she was pretty sure, numbered only seventeen,
THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
369
and that included Aunt Helen and Doctor Gerry. Although
Joe and Sally boasted four youngsters, Stephanie, who lived
at Dedham, had only one; and Jim's Marjorie hadn't married.
Sandy smiled recalling Joe's disappointment when his third
son was born. "I was ashamed to face him, Aunt Sandra,"
Sally had told her. "Every single time he's hoped for a girl,
to name for you. And was he furious when Teresa got ahead
of him! I suppose I'll simply have to try again; though
three's the number I've always planned on."
"Isn't it enough that one of your boys is named for Bob?"
Sandra had asked her.
"Not enough for Joe," responded Sally. "It looks as if I'd
have to keep right on populating the city of Boston till we
get a girl!"
That baby girl was now just three weeks old; but Joe had
promised that both she and her mother would be at Grand-
ma's birthday celebration. Sandra wished that Mr. Bowen
had lived to know about this baby, though he'd been proud
enough at the arrival of his own namesake, Thomas Bowen,
third. She raised herself upon one elbow to see bright sun-
shine streaming through the open window. A lovely day!
Bob's mother would enjoy her ride to Mount Auburn with
Aunt Helen; and Teresa could run out to Roxbury with the
children right after breakfast. It would be her only chance
to say good-bye to dear Aunt Em. The lunch was all ar-
ranged, just as Grandma had ordered it; and Sandra herself

too fresh for every child to have a bite. Eighty-five pink
candles were waiting in their rosebud holders; and Teresa
had insisted that the cake be surrounded by a wreath of her
grandmother's wedding flowers. Nothing had been forgot-
ten, and only this sunny morning was needed to make things
perfect.

to me. Eventually it will go to Stephen, of course; and
Robert is to have his mother's-the mate to yours."
No one, it seemed, had been forgotten, from Doctor Gerry
to the great-grandchildren. Even the faithful servants re-
ceived tokens of affection; and not until all this was over did
Teresa and her husband learn of the contribution to their
hospital-"Another gift from your grandmother," said James,
who had been delegated to "break the news."
But later, after the sandwiches and coffee had disappeared,
and Susan's birthday cake, its eighty-five pink tapers blazing
gaily, had been brought in, wished on and devoured, Teresa
sought out her Uncle James, and said, looking up out of her
wonderful eyes, which, Jim decided as he looked down, had
grown even more lovely with the years: "Mother has told
me how it really happened, Uncle Jim-our getting that
room for the new hospital. Jack's overcome. He'll write,
but he can't seem to express himself just now. It will mean
so much for years and years, not only to us, but to so many
who are poor, and sick, and suffering. You look 'every inch
a King, Uncle Jim dear. So stern and forbidding! Do I
dare kiss you?"

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
"I don't mind if you do," said James, still true to type, but
smiling at her.
...
The sun had long since set, and now that the excitement
was all over, Susan King was beginning to feel tired. Pretty
soon she'd ask one of the boys to take her home. There had
been a lot of confusion, and she felt relieved when the young
folks crowded out into the hall to say good-bye to Stephanie
and her family, who were starting back to Dedham. Susan
sat there alone for a little space, quietly resting, with her eyes
on Helen Langdon and Doctor Gerry. Edward, she mused,
was beginning to look like an old man. Strange to think
that he had wanted to marry Helen and she'd refused him,
yet here they were sixty years later, talking and laughing to-
gether, and having a good time!
...
...
...
Raising her head, Susan smiled at a glimpse of her grand-
son Philip, embracing his Mary in what he no doubt consid-
ered a secluded corner. Henry, she remembered, had once
kissed her when they stood under those very stairs, which
wasn't so retired a place as it appeared to those who were in
it. Probably other young people had kissed there, too; and
if the old house could talk it might have many secrets to
reveal. How quickly time went by! How almost
impossible to realize that eighty-five long years ago she her-
self first saw the light of day under this very roof! Susan
felt glad that one of her sons still lived here that he'd chosen
a wife who hadn't turned up her nose at the neighborhood,
which, thanks to the artists who had bought some of the
best houses and remodeled them, seemed to be slowly return-
ing to its former status. Neither house nor neighborhood
had been stylish enough for Etta Dodge, who had hounded
poor Avery out of it against his will. (It served her right that
she was alone in California-an old woman, and so disagree-
able, according to her brother Howard, that no companion
would stay with her a month!) But Sandra loved the house

as Robert, and Avery, and Helen Langdon had always loved

...
Sandra! Susan King closed her eyes a moment, the
better to bring back a chapter out of the past. Life was very
strange.... It was a pity one couldn't see ahead at times.

Years ago she had been so miserable sure that Rob-

and even Laura, who was never critical, had thought it curi-
ous considering Bob's popularity in his own set. No
doubt they had all made Robert and his young wife very un-
happy. Helen Langdon had told her so quite frankly, and
so had Avery. Yet it was Sandra who had helped poor
Laura pull out of the dreadful mental state she was in after
the war; and Stephen was so grateful to his sister-in-law that
he'd probably lie down and die for her if she asked it. ...
Of course, Stephen had never felt about his brother's mar-
riage as she and James had. James was so sure that Robert
had cut himself adrift from his own people that they would
never have anything in common again; yet twenty years later
Sandra's brother had married James's daughter; and only a
week ago James had observed that "Bob certainly had his
eyes open when he picked a wife!"
And he had, admitted Bob's mother. Despite the differ-
ence in his background and Sandra's, Robert's marriage, in
certain ways, had turned out the most successful of the three;
and when forced into the limelight (which she avoided as
much as possible) there wasn't a more gracious hostess in
Boston than Mrs. Robert King. She was the best-looking of
"the girls," too. Laura, who had been an exquisite blonde
at twenty, and still appeared high-bred, had faded, as blondes
are apt to fade; and the lines wrought by nerves and Jean-

Steve.

THE KINGS OF BEACON HILL
As for Dorothy, she had lived too well and was growing
stout. She pretended not to mind; but Susan felt sure that
it hurt Dorothy a little when James twitted her about her
figure, as he did too often, and then she'd retaliate and say
something that hurt him.
She talked a good deal about
diets, too, which was very boring, especially as she never
stuck to any of them long enough to lose five pounds....
...
But Sandra was better-looking now than she had ever been.
She was neither stout nor thin; and her face was lovely in
maturity, its lines seeming to have been etched only by kind-
ness and good humor. She'd been a wonderful mother, too;
and Robert was absolutely devoted to her. They were all
devoted to her. They admired and looked up to her and
came to her for advice. How, Susan King asked herself, had
Sandra accomplished all this a woman wholly without back-
ground-the daughter of a Roxbury plumber? Surely she had
done nothing spectacular-just lived quietly from day to day,
doing the things she felt were right-being herself.
...
And in an illuminating flash Susan realized that here was
the answer to her question! Sandra had been herself. She
might have no background, but she had that infinitely bet-
ter thing which is called character. "And if we have char-
acter," reasoned Susan King out of the wisdom of eighty-five
long years, “we make our own background, don't we?"
She glanced up, hearing Jeannette exclaim: "Look, Mother!
If only I could paint Aunt Sandra just like that!"
Sandra was standing in the doorway, holding Sally's baby,
another "Sandy Bowen," in her arms. She smiled at her
brother, and her eyes were shining as she said: "I'm so proud,
Joe! Two children named for me-yours and Teresa's;
though I'm afraid such repetitions are confusing to the
family."
"It would be considerably more confusing if she hadn't been

379
named for you," responded Joe. "I told Sally I'd divorce her
if she presented me with another boy."
He put his arm round Sally notwithstanding, and said:
"Go sit down, darling. You'll get tired," while Robert, who
was staring intently at the baby, observed, as if making a
great discovery:
"Do you realize, Joe, that she looks like Sandy?"
A laugh came from his daughter Helen.
"What a wild flight of the imagination, Dad! That in-
fant's the living image of Sally King."
But Sandra, her eyes still shining, told them all: "I don't
care who she looks like. She's my Joe's baby, and she has
my name. Do any of you know, I wonder, what that means
to me?"
She looked at Robert, and his mother saw something pass
between them-only a glance, but such a glance as had some-
times, in rare moments, passed between herself and the in-
articulate Henry King. It brought a sudden dimness to
Susan's vision. Her heartbeats quickened. "Suppose,” she
asked herself, "suppose, like Avery, I should go to sleep to-
night and not wake up? Sandra would never know what
I have come to feel for her...."
And in a breath there swept over Susan King the certainty
that this was too monstrous a thing even to contemplate.
Long ago, through her self-centered blindness, she had
brought great trouble on her son's young wife; and though
they had been friends for many years, she had taken Sandra's
forgiveness for granted, and spoken no word of regret or
appreciation. Was it too late to make amends?
Susan sat straighter, looking indeed the veritable grande
dame.
"Sandy!" she said.
They all turned, even Helen Langdon and Doctor Gerry,

surprised to hear the once-despised nickname on the lips of
Mrs. Henry King of Beacon Hill. But Susan herself never
knew that she had used it. Across the room her eyes met
Sandra's-held them a moment.
"Sandy-my dear-I am proud of you!" said Susan King.
